COUNTY OF CHRSTER. WILD BIRDS PROTRCTION.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIViiN, that the follow- ing is a Copy of an ORDER mad-, by the Secretary of State on the 29th day of March, 1901, with reference to the Protection of Wild Birds in the Administrative County of Chester, viz. WILD BIRD (ENGLkND). THE WILD BIRDS PROTROTION (COUNTY OP CHESTER) WIDER. 1931. Dated 29th March. 1901

In pursuance of the powers conferred on me by the Wild Birds Protection Alts, 1880 to 1898, and upon application by the County Council of the Administrative County of Chester I hereby make the following Order : TITLE.

I. This Order may be cited as " The Will Biris Protec- tion (County of Chester) Order. 1901." BIROS. CLOSE TIME BETENDED.

IT. The time during which the killing or taking of Wild Birds, or any of them. is prohibited by the Act or 1280, shall be extended throughout the County of Chester, so as to be from tbe 2nd da ;7 of March to the 12th day of August In each year. both days Inclusive :

An interesting exhibition consisting of specimens of various handicrafts, amateur work, works of arb, curios, &c., promoted by the Bold-street Wesley Guild, Warrington, was held in the schoolroom on Wednesday and Thursday. The exhibits were the work of com- petitors connected with the Wesleyan schools and con- gregations in the Warrington circuit and they were of a I highly meritorious character. Much skill and pains had ' been bestowed upon the work, some of which possessed 1 great artistic Mer it .Pri ze,s have been awarded for the best exhibits and there was keen competition in several classes. i The opening ceremony was performed by Mrs. T. B. 1 Carter, of Frodsharn,on Wednesdayafternoon. The Rev G. H. Carnson (superintendent Wesleyan minister) pre- sided and in a few introductory remarks said the exhibits were evidence that the members of the Wesley Guild could work. He sometimes met gentlemen who had no hobby or pursuit in life except one and that very often money making. Such a man lost a great deal of the happiness of life. The Wesley Guild, by this exhibi- ! tion wasendeavouring to cultivate a taste for pursuits of a kind which would bring pleasure and happiness to those who took part in them in their spare hours. (Hear, hear.) Mrs. T. B. CARTER. who was cordially received, said it afforded her much pleasure to be with them, especi- ally as her husband had always taken such a deep interest in Bold-street church and schools, and after seeing so many of them at Eversley last summer her own interest in the members of the Mild was awakened. She sin- cerely trusted that the various works and the crafts exhibited might be helpful to many of them in years to come and might be the means of drawing all the mem- bers of the Guild closer together. If they all recognised their talents as great and responsible blessings, with care and culture they would have ten. She had hoped many of them might make names for themselves that would make Warrington proud of them. They could not all be great but they could all be good. (Hear, hear.) In declaring the exhibition open, she wished them all success in their worthy undertaking and in that wish her husband joined. (Applause.) The services of Mrs. Carter were acknowledged on the proposition of the Rev. Theodore BISHOP, seconded by Mr. LITTLER.

Her companion was of a more refined type. He was of no great height, but slender and well-built. His hands and feet were delicate, his face was oval and his features were well-cut. The two were tramps, a.s experienced Mrs Penny had guessed. But only a week before they had been actor and actress in a provincial com- pany that had started from London with what the manager had assured the members were the brightest of prospects. But the tour had not been a success, and after several failures in several towns, the manager had levanted with all the money he could lay his hands on. • He left his company stranded in a dingy Northern town, and as no member had receivecra full salary since the tour commenced, each and everyone was in a dismal plight. Some managed to get back to Lon- don by pawning their clothes. Mat and Judith had hoped to do the same. But an obdurate land- lady had claimed their boxes for arrears of rent and had turned them out on the street. There was nothing for it but to walk tack to the great city, for they had no one to help them. With a Ismail store of money they had set out and had been several days on the road. "A nice cottage," she said to herself, as she walked slowly round Bolt Head one evening; "1 can take in boat-dens, and live quite happily. Meantime I'll be well enough off with Tretnewan. I can manage him; the wife and daughter are not worth considering. Oh. how glad lam that my troubles are all over! For the lest of my life eat well end slew soft, and be respectable. How wise I was to got rid of Met. Yet, poor fellow, be had his redeeming qualities. If he is not dead I must assiet him. But. 1 tepect I'll never see him again. It won't be much less after all," and she chuckled. . .

But Mrs. Bedowen was rather premature in thanking Providence that her troubles Were over. An event occurred that came eN•ening which rather startled her. She had extended her wai:k to the moor beyond Bolt Head, and in her musing had not noticed that a storm was corning on. The sun was sinking red in the west, and lines of black cloud barred the fiery light. The waters were ruffled with foam, and quickly gathering mista eanie streaming down the mountains. Also the wind was rising and swept amongst, the herbage with a low moaning sound. Mrs. Bedowen was not afraid of the weather, however. She had been too inuch in the open to be soared by a thunder- storm. Kilting up the skirts of her new black dre.,,-s----worn in honour of that mythical mariner Jmn Bedowen —she ran down the mountain side 'lad was soon toiling up the rocky path which leads round Bolt Ilea As she bent her energies to the task she did not notice that a man was fol- lowing her. The roar of wind end wave drowned the sound of his feet, and it took all Mrs. Bed.,- wen's time to make her way .against the blast. She never thought of looking round. Yet Nemesis was on her track, in the person of the man she least expected to see. As she reached the highest paint of the path, _ _

jiwt where it rounds to Salcombe' , a blast of -wind nearly.'tore her bonnet from her head. There camo• a flash of livid lightning, and then a 'Rua of tl:mnder whioh driywned the noise of the waves raving below. A drench of min foflowed, and in a moment, as it seemed, Mrs. Bedowen was in the ntith;:t of a storm whicth sto.rtled even her stout heart. Nor was she lers afraid .when a hand sud- denlv graVed her koulder. "Lord Almighty !" shrieked the terrified woman. "Who is itr "Mat, you devil! to--" She whirled round with a yell of dismay. The cry cut through the stormy air like a knife. There, in the livid glare of the lightning, she saw the haggard pallid face of the man she had left for dead in the little provincial inn—the man she had robbed and deserted. After that first Shriek Mrs. Bedowen could only stare blankly at that terrible. face. "You thought to esoa,pe me, did you?" lid sneered hoarsely, and tightened his grasp on her shoulder while the storm raged with fourfold fury.! "I fainted with hunger, and you stole my papers. You devil! I managed to get to Loudon, and

Aat, risen from the dead

found out what you had done. Tremewan has. been asking a..fiter me in my old haunts, and— and—" •

"Leave me alone!" shouted Mrs. Beclowan, try- ing to twist herself free. "I have had my own way, aid I'll keep What I've got." 'You'll not. I will twist your neck," and ha 'wrenched her arm.

" Bea4l" she yelled, and tearing herself free, made a gmh at his throat. "])o you think I'm not stronger than you? I'll ehoke you 1" Far from trying to get away, Mat drew her to "We'll go over together," he said, with a peal of terrible laughter; "one in life and one in death."

"Let go--let go !" cried Mrs. Bedowen terrified. Her strength, great as it was, was as nothing against the nervous farce exerted by the man she had wronged. He dragged her, inch by inch, nearer to the low wall which protected the path. Below were the hungry waves and the black. sharp-pointed rocks. They would fall hundreds of feet, and be dashed to pieces: And to die just when she had gained all that made life worth living? Closing her mouth, Mrs. Beclawen fought in desperate silence. Her hair gave way, her bonnet came off, her dress bore, but she fou-At and bit and scratched and kicked for dear life. Mat, also, was silent save .for a sobbing breath. He had his lean hands on her throat, and she made desperate efforts to get free. The mist came rol- ling down from the heights above and blotted out the path. In that white wetness they struggled in silence. In their ears the sea roared far below. The storm was dying away, the wind had swept away over-sea, and in the dense choking fog, the man and woman strovz, the one tlb kill the other. For a moment Mat's strength gave way. It was Judith's opportunity. She wrenched herself to one side, and leaving part of her dress in his grip, stumbled blindly down the path. Mat gasped for a moment, then drew a revolver, and rushed after her. _ _ _ _

"I'll kill you—l'll kill you!" he shouted, an 4 lunged on. The mist was sail around, a thick white pall over the rocks. A silence broken only by the beating of the waves prevailed. Then there came a. cry of terror—of agony,--the sound of falling rocks— a heavy plunge. And the silence shut down once more. And the niiists grew more and more dense. Only the waves rejoiced, as though they had at last seized their expected prey.

A meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- street, on Wednesday evening tinder the auspices of the Manchester and District Association for the Abolition .of the State Regulation of Vice. There was a very small attendance, the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) presi- dinu6. It was not right that any law should be passed protecting vice and enabling men -to sin with impunity.—An address was delivered by the rev. J. P. Gledstone, of London, who took for his subject " State Regulation of vice ; what it is, and why it should be abolished." The Rev.gentleman dealt with the question at length and pointed out,that.his.hearers as citizens were more or less responsible for all legislature whether it was for good or evil. PADGATE.

SUCCESSFUL CONCERT.--A very succeissful concert was given in the Clrirch schools on Saturday evening under the direction of Mrs. Jennings, in aid of the funds of the new infants school at Padgate, and the Woolston school. The programme was sustained by a number of Liverpool ladies and gentlemen, and gave the greatest pleasure to the large audience, encores being frequently accorded. A pianoforte solo was very creditabl y executed by Mrs. F. D. Austin, and songs were rendered with good effect by Miss M. Roberts, Mrs. W. Austin, Mr. F. D. Austin, Mr. W. Roberts, and Mr. Paul Waring. A quartette "A Royal Queen" was very well received, and the recitations by Mr. E. Coops called forth high en- comiums. Mr. C. Harris provided the humorous element, and succeeded in keeping the audience in .a very happy mood.

FULL OF PRETTY GIRLS! Coventry's Gallant Reputation.

Coventry work-girls spend their lives in buildings for the most part well-eesigned, clean, and airy, where work is not unpleasant. This is perhaps why Coventry lasses rival in physique and attractiveness of appearance any of their sex.

Thus gallantly boasts the "Midland Daily Telegraph," which publishes the portrait of a typical Coventry girl, Miss Eva Harper, the daughter of a highly respect- , , ~- able family well known in the neigh- bourhood of its /4-j',-,•,. ~, ' ~. • ---, home at 64, George- a• it'f' 4 c.-,, ,_, \ street, Coventry. At one time in a 7 slowly dying con- . dition, Miss ,i• ~,t .- . I

symptoms of anw- ' A Coventry zag.3lal:er. mia and to experi- ence the fainting fits shortness of breath, and ' lack of appetite, which, in spite of the most skilful medical treatment, were soon to make my life a burden. If I went upstairs it was with difficulty, I was so weak and my breath was so short. My cheeks and lips were colourless, and I suffered much from palpitation of the heart. I was under two doctors and took ever so many different kinds of medicines, but I continued in this state for over eighteen months, and was sent away from work. The first box of Dr. Williams' Pink pills did not seem to do me much good, but when I had taken the second I was able to eat the first meal I had really enjoyed for over twelve months. I took one box a week for a month, and at the end of that time I was sufficiently recovered to be able to go back to work. Six boxes in all restored me completely to health, and I feel as well now as ever I did in my life."

A pale cheek, dull eyes, poor complexion, lost breathlessness and palpitating heart denote poor blood. The condition leads to consumption: it is too danger- ous too neglect; too dangerous to play with, with substitutes. Rheumatism, paralysis, St. Vitus' dance, and other diseases of blood and nerves have been cured by the genuine pills. Do not risk your life with pur- gatives or substitutes. Miss Harper's fortunate recovery has raised her family from mourning to joy.

THE OPENING OF PARLIAMENT.

His Majesty seems rightly determined to carry Dtit with proper pomp and pageant the duties which fall to the lot of a modern monarch under our own constitution; for on Tuesday. for tht third time since his accession, he drove in state tc. open the new Session of the Houses of Parliament, and present in person the Address from the Throne. The procession was as interesting and impressive as ever, and drew large crowds to the Mall and lion the route, to watch it and cheer the King, Who seems thoroughly to have recovered from his slight illness. The route was lined with troops under the command of Major-General Oliphant, who made his first public appearance as gen?ral commanding the Home district in succession to Sir Henry Trotter, and all the arrangements were well carried out. The King, accompanied by Queen Alexandra, drove in the now familiar State Coach, drawn by the celebrated team of eight cream., and the Royal carriage was preceded by five others, each drawn by six horses, and conveying the various members of his Majesty's Household. Reaching the Houses of Parliament, the King robed in the Prince's Chamber, and a procession was formed, the Duke of Devonshire bearing the Imperial Crown and the Duke of Norfolk (Earl Marshal) and the Marquess of Cholmondeley (the Lord Great Chamberlain) walking ire- mediately in front of the Marquess of Londonderry, who bore the Sword of State

DUKE OF ROXBURGH;

MR. GRETTON,

IN THE LORDS. IN THE COMMONS. in front of his Majesty, by whose side walked tht Queen. • The Prince of Wales, also, drove to the House in state, and sat on the right of the Throne. The King read the address, and then re- turned by the same route to the Palace about three 91;elock. The reply to/the address was moved in the b Pl3er House by the Duke of Roxburghe, seconded Y the Earl of Leitrim, and in the Lower House Was Moved by Mr. Gretton and seconded by Mr. Greville. The present session promises to be a busy one. First, the Venezuelan situation will be threshed out, and then the Guard' scandal, and \ then will come the Education Bill for London and the Irish Land Bill, while a Rediatribution Bill has been hinted at by Mr. Austen Chamberlain, and an Alien Immigration Bill is eagerly hoped for.

THE MARCorn sTAT;ON AT GIBRALTAR.

Wireless telegraphy, or " ethergraphy," as many People are beginning to call it. is without doubt the great thing of the near future, marvellous as such a statement would have seemed two or three years back. However, Mr. Marconi has made almost incredible advances during that time, and less than a month ago achieved the crowning triumph of sending messages across the Atlantic. Now it wants but little to render the system per- fect, the question of " tapping " messages being the greatest difficulty, and it is likely to be univer- 'sally adopted before long. The latest station Which has been selected for the installation of the Marconi system, is Gibraltar, and here it should be Invaluable, especially in time of war or isolation.

Not only will it be in direct communication with headquarters in London, but it will also serve as a handy and convenient medium of communication between fleets and ships, who do not know each other's whereabouts. In fact, under such circum- stances its true value can scarcely be estimated. The exact spot selected for the installation is the signal station at Europa Point, the southernmost extremity of Gibraltar, and during his recent visit Mr. Brodrick visited and approved it as Seeretary for War, while inspecting the defences of the Rock.- Mr. Marconi returned from his triumphs in the west last week, and was honoured by a special audience with the King, who is greatly interested in his great discovery.

THE LEYTON HORROR.

Seldom, if ever, has there been a case of such atrocious and cold-blooded murder as that for which " Edwards " (Owen is his proper name) will be hanged on Tuesday week at Wandsworth gaol, and public opinion is entirely against a repreive. The defence set up, the only one possible under the circumstances, was one of insanity, and, despite " Edwards's " terrible family history of drink and madness, it cannot be considered proven in his case. The line between sanity and insanity is a most difficult one to draw, but little sympathy can be extended to criminal who can so deliberately plan in the closing days of a term of imprisonment, a scheme of crime with a view to getting a fresh start in the world and carry it out so promptly and unerringly. There can be no doubt that the deaths of the Darl-y family were the direct result Of Edward's cogitations in prison, where he evolved this plan to get hold of a shop for nothing by murdering its inmates, and, had it not been for the breaking of a pane of glass at 89, Church Road, Leyton. it is highly probable that not only would the crime not have been brought home to him, but that Mr. Garland, Mrs. Garland, and possibly others would have been added to his list of victims. Foiled in his attempt to get hold of the Darbys' shop in Camberwell after the murders owing to the landlord's refusal to take him as tenant, "Edwards" immediately laid his plans with a view to acquiring another shop by similar means. Hence his murderous assault on Mr. Garland, who must consider himself a lucky man to ba alive to-day. Edwards, whose age is forty, has passed

" Edwards" it is interesting to note, was identified as an habitual criminal under many other names by the Galion system of identification by finger prints, which is proving as valuable in 1 England as the Berti'lon system in France. BRITAIN'S BIGGEST LOCOMOTIVE.

MRS. DARBY.

It is generally believed that railway comp..":"...!Z;s are now in the throes of hesitation whether the future must be one of electrified railways, or whether the vast improvements eonstaatly being made in the steam engine will justify the retention of the old motive power. Railway companies are conservative, and do not relish the provect of their none too big dividends being hopelessly swallowed up fo- a very long time to come by electrification. Moreover, the steam engine has always been found reliable and safe, so that it may be very readily believed that if universal electrifi- cation does come, it will not be until motors much more reliable than those of the present are made. One of the latest improvements in steam locomo- tives is an engine designed and constructed at Swindon works, and placed on the Great Western system. It marks the limit in height and width available on English railways, and is probably the largest and most powerful engine working in Great Britain. It is now being utilised for the Cornish-

THE NEW GREAT WESTERN PASSENGER ENGINE

THE LARGEST LOCOMOTIVE IN GRFAT BRITAIN. man" expresses, which are accelerated so as to cover the distance of 118? f miles between Padding- ton and Bristol in two hours and ten minutes. a timing which gives an average running speed of sixty miles an hour. The boiler of the engine is of very large dimensions, and the tender has a carrying capacity of 4,000 gallons of water and five tons of coal. The total weight of engine and tender is 105 tons. A novel feature of this class of engine is the provision of a duplicate set of driving wheels, which enables the locomotive to be used for hauling the heaviest freight trains over the many severe gradients of the Great Western line. At present the services of two locomotives are required on several sections of the line. There are great hopes of a new engine to be put on the Great Eastern shortly. It has been designed to take heavy suburban tratile, and is expected to attain a speed of thirty miles per hour in thirty seconds. The Great Eastern Company say that if this engine comes up to expectations they will abandon the thought of electrification for ever.

A FAMOUS OLD JUG.

The famous West Mailing Eizabethan jug. th property of tile vicar and (quire:, wardens of West Mailing, Kent, which was sold by auction on Thursday for the purpose of raising money for the church fund,, ha been prt:.served in West Mailing since 1 I. West IV.:,llhw lies I).tween Sevenoaks and Maidstone, andr wit h East Mailing i a place of interest to arch:‘oogists and historians as marking the site of a priniiiive Sayon tribe, the Mallingas The Benedictine nunnery founded there in 1090 by the great Gundalf. Bi,hop of Rochester, was held by the Abbess by the singular tenure of ten pounds of wax and one wild boar from the oak woods that surrounded the convent. annually de- livered to the Bishop as a token or her subjection

THE WEST MALLING FLAGON WHICH WAS SOLD ON T lIU RS DAY.

to his see. ale silver-mounted stone-ware jugs, of which this specimen at West Mailing is so beautiful and rare an example, were much in vogue during the -whole. of the sixteenth century for domestic and, as this specimen . shows, probably also for ec:esiastical purposes. Collectors of ancient English ware have always been especially alert in their search after Elizabethan specimens, and once in a while they have an opportunity of acquiring examples. Such jugs as the \Vest Mailing specimen were generally imported from Germany, and were "Then decorated and mounted by our own English silvPrsmiths. The earliest of them seem to have been in use in the time of Henry VIII. The West Mailing specimen is an extreumly fine and rich example and has a repouss4 cover with a broad engraved neck-band, and a repousse base. At the junction of the neck and body is another narrower but richly-worked band, and this is connected with the base by four upright bars, the upper portion of which are human half-figures.

EVENTS OF THE

The Examiner, Friday, February 20, 1903.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

During this month many of the tailors and kessmakers who owe their reputations to the exeellency of their cut and fit, and wleese usual charges match their renown in strength and height so far relax their rules as to offer very reduced prices for a few weeks. This annual practice is done in order that they may continue to give employment to the large staff of works during this so-called dull season when there is a sort of lull in the sartorial world pending the arrival of the spring modes. I should, therefore, earnestly counsel the woman of good taste but limited means who can afford but few gowns, but must have these as near per- fection a q possible, so far as the cut and style are concerned, to avail herself of this annual opportunity and invest in a thoroughly good costume which will prove a source of satisfaction to herself and all beholders during the rest of the year. If one waits for another couple of months till the new modes are fairly launched one has also to wait the dressmaker's pleasure till the new gown is unfait accompli, while the price is nearly double what would be asked now.

A SMART TOILETTE.

A very smart toilette nas been sketched, the material in which it is expressed being a smooth surfaced black cloth of a firm yet light make. The skirt is well cut but quite plain but is full round the feet and tight fitting round the hips in the manner approved of at the present. Indeed, in spite of all that has been said and written the plain skirt of irreproachable cut is likely to continue fashionable through this year. It is a style eminently fitted to show a really good figure to advantage. The bodice of this costume fits the figure closely at the back and is stitched into a belt which is broader at the back than the front and comes to a point in the middle of the back much in the style of a Swiss belt. The cape like collar and deep cuffs are of red and white embroidery and are finished by tassels, while a row of tiny little gold buttons fasten the front. The hat has a black nelvet fiat crown, the brim being of stitched

A toilette of this style carries no date with it, and the wearer thereoi can indulge in the comforting assurance oi being well and tastefully dressed. In the world of millinery there is not mud to be noted save in the fragile and delicately tinted " creations " worn by those who are fortunate enough to be able to escape the worm of an English winter by sojourning in Egypt or on the Continent. ===== 0002601_19030220_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S GHAT..

The London season does not commence until Ma y but many leading people in Society always return to town for the opening of Parliament. It seems strange that the wealthy and leisured classes should select for a sojourn in town those months during which the country is most attractive, but from now until the end of July that part of the West End which- is given up to the fashionable world, will present a crowded and busy aspect. The Lenten season is not now the period of abstinence it used to be, and the leading modistes, who are already planning new toilettes to be worn at the Courts, expect to be fully employed. They were, however, not at all pleased with the Royal intimation that the peeresses might wear their, Coronation robes at the opening of Parliament. Some who do not appear to the best advan- tage in the robes, availed themselves of the option to appear in a Court dross without a train, but on the whole this new departure meant the loss of many orders to the fashionable dressmakers.

Easter will be two weeks later than last year, and in this it accords with the opening of Parliament which is also two or three weeks later than usual. Easter, which falls on the 12th April, may however be as late as April 25th, or as early as March 22nd. If regarded purely as a holiday, a late Easter is preferable, as the chances are not only in favour of better weather, but the advance of Spring gives a more delightful appearance to the country. It has even been suggested that, instead of continuing a •movable feast, the Church should give Easter day a settled date, and that it should be the third Sunday in Apr 11. In former times there was an officer of the Royal Court who was known as the " King's Cock Crowe-r " and, during the season of Lent, he used to crow the hour of the night, instead of calling it. This was in accordance with the usual custom, during Lent, which was no doubt intended to remind people of the effect which the third crowing of the gook had upon, St. Peter. This however was put a stop to by George 1. whose acquaintance with old English customs was somewhat limited. On the first Ash Wednesday after his accession, the officer entered and crowed" past ten o'clock " whilst the Court was assembled at supper. The astonished King mistook the crow for an insult, but though it was explained to him, that it was all in. ac- cordance with Court customs, it was never afterwards repeated. The recent exhibition at the Crystal Palace shows that great progress is being made in improving the motor car. It is still no doubt a long way off that near approach to perfection for Which many people are waiting before investing in one, but the manufacturers seem at last to be succeeding in making them a little less costly than formerly. When they first came out, some people were sanguine enough to think that every family would soon be able to have its motor car, and to run it for next to nothing, as compared with the cost of a horse and carriage. We are still a long way off the realization of such dreams, but it is said that the great Mr. Edison, who has achieved so many wonders, will sooner or later pro. duce an electric storage battery that will bring them I within range of possibility. , There is, at any rate, very Ilittle doubt that the noisy, evil-smelling vehicles, will before long give place to quiet, smooth-running, and let us hope comparatively inexpensive cars. Stoles no longer appear on tea-gowns and Empire frocks only, but they are finding a place on many of the newest evening gowns, blouses, coats, amid coatees. Falling from the shoulders, to almost reach the knees, they are often elaborately embroidered, the edges being run in and out with chiffon, or baby ribbons, and the ends fringed. It is, in this particular instance, skill, rather than costly fabric that tells, and to those women who embroider well, these stoles open up wide possi- bilities.

The sash, sometimes of chiffon, but more frequently of chins ribbon, six to eight inches in width,still appears on many of the smartest ball gowns, to which it affords a delightful finish. The loops are carelessly knotted, and the ends fall always to the edge of the skirt. In belts there is a decided change. For some time past the rule has been that they should mount high at the hack, and dip down low and narrow in front. Now we do not seem to be able to build them wide enough in front. The latest of these belts are really corselets, most carefully shaped, draped, and boned. They are by no means so becoming to the average figure, as the belts high at the back, and dipping down well in front, and will probably enjoy but a short vogue. . .

Women wile .store of - ancient tieasures should promptly unearth them, for there probably never was a time when old world articles of attire were of greater service. For example. there are the old lace lappets which we can arrange in our hair, and be quite up-to-date; the stiff little bows and rosettes we can sew on to our gowns and shoes; the deep lace capes coming to a point in front, so much sought after at the moment; the lace tucker which is finishing off the Mcollotage of all the best evening bodices; and the wide lac 6 sleeves which appear to such adyantage under the bell-shaped sleeve of the present day. For these, and many more old-world items, there is a use just now, and the opportunity is too good a one to lose. The fashionable writing paper is either soft grey or blue, the stamping being carried out in white. Plain white writing paper has not enjoyed much favour for some time past, though it is still of course, much used by the ordinary world. Quite small sheets and enve- lopes, are not correct; For some time they have been increasing in size, and happily, as even in these days of brief letter writing, the one time microscopic sheets were of little use while only the most scanty address could be got, together with the necessary stamp, upon the envelope. The craze of sealing one's letters with crest or monogram,still exists,the colour of the sealing- wax.exactly matching that of the paper. It is curious to find how few people there are who possess letter-scales. Of course in these days when four ounces of letter go for a penny, the perplexities of life in this particular direction are almost over, still even yet occasions remain. when the absence of a letter-balance causes much anxiety and•bother. The usual war out of the difficulty is to employ the kitchen scales, but these unhappily are often superior to mere accuracy, or they decline to be put in motion by anything less than, say, a quarter of a pound. It is seldom one finds letter scales among Christmas, wedding, or birthday gifts, yet it is a want that is occasionally felt in almost every household. MADGE,

Colonel A.S.Sadler, the popular member for Middles- borough, told an amusing story the other evening (says the "Westminster Gazette") at the local Conservative Club that has a pungent bearing on the vexed question of "ragging." His son is an army officer who dis- tinguished himself on more than one occasion during the recent South African campaign. Young Sadler, like all newly joined men, "had to 4.10 through the usual routine of horseplay." " 'Matters became so unbearable one night that my son came to the conclusion that he had suffered long enough. He threw one of his tormentors over the billiard table, placed the second on the sick list for six weeks, and blacked the eye of the third." And, added Colonel Sadler, in his inimitable way, mold a roar of laughter. "he has had a very pleasant life in his regiment ever since."

OUR LOCAL CaURCHES. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

An Open Meeting.

Wycliffe Church was filled on Sunday afternoon on the occasion of an open meeting in connection with the P.S.A. Society, when the members were allowed to bring their wives and lady friends. Mr. Bindloss presided and briefly introduced Captain Harvey, of the Liverpool Seamen's Friend Society, who delivered an address in the absence of Mr. H. Clark. The speaker based an interesting and instructive address on "Opportunity." The Warrington Male Voice Choir sung several selec- tions, in an efficient manner. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., conducted, and the solo part in "Psalm 42," was sung by Mr. Hassall.—The Bev. John Yong° briefly addressed the meeting.—A collection was taken on behalf of the P.S.A. funds.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
Ellesmere-strect Mission.

Councillor W. Brough preached at a special service held in this mission on Sunday evening in aid of the choir funds. The service was for the most part choral, being entitled" Heaven our Home." As a text upon which to base his discourse the preacher chose the words "And they shall come from the east, and from the west, and from the north, and from the south, and shall sit down with Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob, and shall be at rest." Solos were rendered by Mrs. Davenport, Mr. Warburton,. and Mr. Drinkwater, and a duet by Miss Humphreys and Mr. Drinkwater. The choir contributed nine or ten pieces, all bearing on the subject. Mr. Anderson officiated as organist and Miss Patty Taylor accompanied on the pianoforte.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
Evangelistic Services at Buckley-street.

A fortnight's mission is being conducted in this place of worshiP, by Captain W. and Mrs. Clark, of Liverpool. A meeting of welcome to the Missioner and his wife was held on Saturday evening. Mr. J. J. Fairhurst (president), on behalf of the church, welcomed Captain and Mrs. Clarke, and said he trusted they would make themselves feel at home. The workers of the. church would put forward every effort to make the mission a great success. in reply, the Missioner expressed the pleasure it gave his wife and himself to be 'so heartily welcomed, and urged them to work diligently in bring- ing people to the mission. There were large congrega- tions on Sunday, when Mrs. Clarke preached morning and evening. Yn. the morning she took as her text the words " Unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given," and in the evening " Verily, verily." The sermons were full of earnest gospel appeals, and a great impression for good was made upon the congregations. Captain Clarke addressed the Sunday school in the afternoon. At the evening service the choir rendered a special anthem, which was greatly appreciated. Mr. Albert E. Trow was the organist. The services during the week have been largely attended.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
Golborne-street Baptist Church.

The Pastor continued hie,discourses on Sunday even- ing last, and preached on the "Incarnation" or "How God became Man." Groat interest has been aroused, and on Sunday the chapel was crowded, many being unable to find seats. Having appealed for the sympathy and attention of the congregation in discussing such a metaphysical subject, the preacher went on to say that the fundamental assumption of the Christian religion was that Jesus Christ was the Incarnate God. If that assumption was false then the whole superstruc- ture erected thereon must fall to the ground. In the opinion of many minds that view of the person of Christ was an incredible andabsurd one and altogether unthinkable. He ventured to say however that such a conclusion could only be arrived at from a false promise namely that God and man were essentially unlike each other. The idea of the incarnation was not a modern idea and in the mytholgies of Greece and Rome tem- porary incarnations of deities formed the basis of many myths and legends. Those incarnations were, of coarse, gross and revolting as compared with the Christian view of the incarnation but they served to illustrate the fact that an incarnation was not foreign to the human mind and it has still to be proved that such an idea was incredible or unthinkable. The assumption that, man and God were essentially unlike was altogether untenable and the whole religious hietorY of mankind bore witness to the kinship of God and man. Worship was only made possible on the sup- position that the One above was in some respects at least similar to the one below. The study of scripture emphasised that truth clearly and that being so then they might safely infer that there must of necessity be some kind of Revelation. The God who would not reveal Himself could ndt be all good and the God who could. not reveal Himself would not be all powerful. The dis- course was listened to with rapt attention and next 'Sunday evening Mr. Dunnico will devote the whole of the sermon to the objections raised against the doctrine.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S FORTHCOMING CONCERT.

On Tuesday next the Warrington Musical Society gives its final concert of the season in the Parr Hall. As will be seen from the advertisement on our front page, the artistos will be Madame Alice- Esty, Miss Lilian Hovey and Mr. Charles Saunders,—soprano, con- tralto and tenor respectively—each and all of whom have already won favour with Warrington audiences. Mr. Ivor Foster (the bass vocalist) and Mr. William Henley, solo violinist, will, if we mistake not, both appear at these concerts for the first time. The programme is of a miscellaneous character and includes amongst its novelties two items of-music from tbepen of an English composer who is rapidly rising to front rank—Dr. Elgar. One of these is the "Coronation Ode" which was so much and so effectively en evidence last year. The work was composed for the Grand Opera Syndicate for state performance at Covent Garden and is dedicated "by special permission" "To His Most Gracious Majesty KING EDWARD Vii., by His Majesty's loyal anddceoted Scrrants and Subjects, ARTRUR CHMSTOPHER BEN- SON AND EDWARD ELGAR."

It is divided into six movements (I) Introduction, soli and chorus, "Crown the King," (2) Chorus " Daughter of Ancient Kings," (3) Solo (bass) and chorus (tenors and basses) " Britain, ask of thyself," (4) Soli (soprano and tenor) " Bark, upon the hallowed air," and Quartet (four principal voices) " Only let the heart be pure," (5) Quartet and chorus (unaccompanied) "Peace, gentle peace," and (6) Finale (contralto solo I and tatti) Land of Hope and Glory." The score, in . addition to the usual orchestra, soli and chorus parts, includes a military band, organ, and harp, all of which material,except the military bit nd, will be requisitioned lon Tuesday next. The writer of the annotated I programme of The Halle Concerts Society -speaks of I this important work in terms of such -warm appreci- ation as to savour somewhat of fulsome adulation. But without indulging in any local pre-judgment of the work it may be interesting to quote a few sentences from these analytical notes. In regard to the closing portions of the first number, "Crown the King," we learn that the march theme in which its varied measures culminate, represents "a universal prayer innate with the feeling of triumphal trust, the all conquering spirit of the nation's faith in God and itself," the final bars being "wonderfully illustrative of this state of mind which seems to be the spirit animating and permeating the whole of this first movement; the daring to hope being justified by a strong faith as expressed by the opening phrase, and the hope and the faith being based upon the solid and practical foundation of a great tenacity of purpose expressed by the slow chromatic movement in the inner parts and hams." The four-part chorus "Daughter of " the note-writer regards as "very short nel very sweet," the whole munber being "replete with the most chaste and poetic feeling." "Britain ask of thyself "is in the strongest possible contrast, being,(for male voices only) "martial, rugged, and strop.'" e ending,with its fff for fullest orchestra with alCd thrums, trumpets, cymbals and military band, " pealing forth victory!" Hark upon the hallowed air " we are told that we have "a moat delicious piece of instru- mental writing, .descriptive, or more appropriately illustrative, of the beauties and attributes ot music. Dr. Iggar is certainly to be congratulated upon the skill and taste displayed in this most ideal number," and referring to the musical phrase to which the words " Only let the heart be pure '. are set, and to its treat- ment in full orchestral form, our author con- tinues : "special attention is drawn to this as to a rare inspiration, executed as only the greatest and most refined master of his art could do it." The contralto solo " Land of hope and glory" which forms the Finale has for its fundamental theme the fine melody which concludes the first number, this theme being in fact "the central as well as the culminathw idet of the Coronation Ode. To an overwhehning march rhythm, the proud and confident pulsation of the hearts of millions of happy and loyal subjects, the whole nation rises and sings with all the enthusiasm born of love for country and King— 'Hark a mighty nation maketh glad reply; Lo, our lips are thankful, 10, our hearts are high ! Hearts in hope uplifted, loyal lips that sing; Strong in faith and freedom,we have crowned our King ! —a triumphal hymn, the conclusion of which unites t several themes in a. laurel wreath of fame with which Dr. Eiger in this magnificent work has crowned himself :Is well as his King and his nation !"

ME

===== 0002601_19030220_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
THERE was absolutely THE OPENING OF nothing in the KINd& THE SESSION, Speech which has not

been anticipated in. these columns down even to the Bill which is. to deal with the adulteration of butter, and which will probably be slaughtered somewhere towards the end of the Session. There are State secretg which can be kept, no doubt, but those are not the secrets of the Speech from the Throne. The KING cannot be said to have been provided with a lively or encouraging document with which to open the third Session of what is really his second Parliament, though there seems to be a pretty general disposition to speak of it as his first. His MAJESTY rejoiced with all of us over the settle- ment which has enabled us to bring the Venezuelan blockade to a close, and probably he regrets as much as any of us that a blockade policy should ever have been adopted, though there are things that may not be said in a KING'S Speech. What was said in the Speech concerning the European provinces of Turkey was the reverse of reassuring. His MAJESTY trusts that he may be able to give his hearty support to the joint proposals of Russia and of -Austria-Hungary, but he does not venture to sp-ak oil t probability that:these propo- sals will beiaccepted. To enter into negotiations with Turkey ii any direction whatever is to run up against the stone wall, as once more appears from his MAJESTY'S reference to the fruitless at- tempts to arrive at a delimitation agreement in regard to the bounderies of the tribal countries adjoining Aden. "An Injun's a snake and always a snake," says the American poet, speaking from an ingrained prejudice which;was only very:partially well-founded. The Turk is always an impracticable as well as an " unspeakable " person. He can never be induced to do anything reasonable until he is" man-handled," to use Mr. KIPLING'S ex- pressive word. The KING'S Speech threw the jfirst glimmer of light on our reasons for the cap- ture of Kano. The EMIR of that place, it seems, has been guilty of some hostile action or other. I trust, said his Majesty, " that It will now become possible to proceed in safety with the delimitation of the boundary between my terri- tory of Northern Nigeria and the adjoining pos- sessions of the French R ipublic." Placing two and two together, we arrive at the conclusion that there has been another simaltsneous rush for territorial expansion which has reproduced some- thing like the Fashoda rivalry betwesn England and France, and that the E air of Kano has been found to be in the possession of a post that may be of great assistanc to ourselves in the winning of the game. Sir HENRY :CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN probably hit the right nail on the head when he said last night that Sir F. LUOARD, as High Com- missioner, has planned and carried out this sate

===== 0002601_19030220_art0074.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
\

===== 0002601_19030220_art0075.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
•

===== 0002601_19030220_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
prise entirely on his own account, and without in- spiration from home. it will probably be dis - -covered some day that Sir F. LIICIARD is the most irresponsible and therefore the most dangerous of all our "prancing pro-Consuls." He has on more than one occasion before this taken such action -Independently, as he would not have been per- mitted to take if tie had asked for instructions.

Sir HENRY CA.MPBELL-BANNERMAX made a most excellent speech in the debate on the Address, dis- tinguished not only by good points but by fine statesmanship. He was easily able, of course, to score off the Recess Speeches of members of his MAJP.sTv's Government. There was poor Lord CRA/CBORNE, for instance. He had thought it Worth while to make the remark; that it is as Much the duty of the Empire to protect the poor fishermen in Trinidad as the South African millionaires. This is what Mr. BALFOUR calls an ",unimpeachable 'copybook platitude." But it was a disastrously unfortunate admission to come from 'the Under-Secretary for Foreign Affairs. It re- vealed, as "0.-B." said, the real objects of the late War. This led, in the course of a most interesting and useful speech, to a plea that the two parties shall now work for the same common purpose in South Africa. We ought to be now per- mitted, Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN thinks, to speak out our minds frankly, and to discuss matters of policy without being Blibiooted to taunt and reproach. This also might, be deseribed as a copybook platitude, if the strong- est man in the Ministry had not been accustomed to describe his opponents as "traitors," and if the ParstE MprrsTEß had net excused manifestations of popular violence on the ground that it is dangerous to disagree with an excited public. The excited public has come to its, senses since then. There is an old story about two Greek orators. " If you speak like that," said one, " tbe people Will go mad and kilt yen." "And then," was the 431)137, "they will grow sane again and kill you." 'The application is close, but we trust that Mr. lIALFOUR'S invitation to the expression of political Opinions by means of half-bricks will not be fol- lowed when the inevitable revulsion comes. We Should, as the Liberal reader remarks, be able to speak our minds frankly without danger either of verbal or physical assault.

The Venezuelan difficulty, now happily settled, has once more brought prominently before the attention of the British nation the Monro 3 doc- trine, which has played in the past so large a part in all the negotiations between this country and the United Sttites. And it is, perhaps, wise ton the nation should make itself acquainted with the nature of that doctrine, and how far it affects the Position of this country in its reiations with th American nation. Captain A. T. Mahan, th. 3 ominent naval writer, has, in a recent article in the "National Review," traced the history and development of the doctrine from the early days of the nineteenth century, and he lays down the Principle that the doctrine in its inception and its expression is a protective measure: That is a po- oition which the British Government does and Which Eaglishmen generally can, accept. The doctrine had its birth in the desire of the United States to be independent and to be free of the dis- Putes of the European system. The America,' People had broken loose from 'European control, and they were resolved that, so far Thash, they I were able to secure, the influences T"oh they dreaded should not be extended to tne metriment of the independent position of the R - rile, and that no part of the American continent peould become a pawn in the game of the Eura t0.:44 sYstem, transferable at the will of European archies That this policy has developed is in OU • To some extent American intervention b 3 Was inconsistent with the earlier, though not Was the later, interpretations of the doctrine. ' as 'Ong as the doctrine remains protective teat Britain may endorse it. Her position in Canada makes her interests, to some extent, coio- cident with those of the United States. Wee the national sentiment of America to render tie doctrine aggressive, however, the coincidence of British and American interests would end.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

HOPE HALL, RUNCORN. Special services were held at, the above Hall on Sun- day, Mr. Blinston and several Widnes friends delivering interesting addresses. A lantern lecture was given on "The life of Joseph" in the evening. Mr. Jones, missioner, had charge of the lantern, and Miss A. Jones presided at the organ.

SMALLPDX AT Hikurox BROOK.

Two cases of smallpox have been reported from Hal- ton Brook during the week-end. The patients are both married women. The first case was notified on Saturday and the second on Sunday. They have been removed to the Moore hospital, and the usual precautions have been taken to prevent any further spread of the disease.

'A FATAL FALL:

An inquiry into, the circumstances attending the death of Gertrude Dale (18), daughter of William Dale, Wes- ton Point, was conducted by Mr. R. .Dobson, district coronei-, at the Weaver Hotel, Weston Point, on Monday afternoon.—The evidence Showed that the deceased was in the employ of a Manchester gentleman eighteen months ago. On August 30th, 1901, she was sent into the box room which had a skylight in the floor, Deceased did not knowof the skylight, and fell through breaking her spine. Deceased's father Sued the employer and obtained £225 damages. The body, according to Dr. MacLennan, was completely paralysed below the injury, and there was no possibility of recovery.—A verdict was returned of" Death from misadventure." TREAT TO OLD FOLKS AT HALTON.'

The annual treat to old women and widows of Halton was held in the girl's school on Friday evening. About 40 sat down to tea, the oldest of whom was 85. The following ladies presided at the tables :—Mrs. Wray, Mrs. Stokes, Miss Wright, Miss Ackerley, and Mrs. Walker. Messrs. P.aud R. Wright and Miss Stokes also • attended to the wants of the guests. After tea a capital. entertainment was provided. Humorous speeches were delivered by the Revs. G. D. Wray and S. Wilkinson (Timperley). The guests afterwards indulged in games. and then an old country dance. They were provided with coffee and other refreshments, and each was also presented with a quarter of a pound of tea. About 25 sick people and others were not forgotten, and a share of the good things was sent to them. A vote of thanks, was, on the motion of Mr. Wray, passed to Mr. and Mrs. T. Wright for their continued interest in the annual treat, and the proceedings closed with the singing of the National Anthem. RUNCORN "Co-or" ANNUAL SOCTAL. SONS OF' TEMPERANCE.

On Saturday evening the usual meeting of the above Orderswas held in the Greenway-road School, under the presidency of Mr. George Powell. After the ordinary business had been transacted Mr. Thomas J.Holt,district secretary, said that two yearsago, Mr. William Gledhill. of Hull, the dhief officer of the Order, when assuming the position at West Hartlepool threw out a challenge to the Order to increase their membership by 10,000 adults in the first two years of the centut.y. A few days ago he (Mr. Holt) received a communication from Mr. Gledhill that they had accomplished their object; their increase for the two years being 10,019. The Order is an American importation, and was established in this country in the year 1849. The members have steadily increased, their being now 600 divisions with 60,000 adult members and 430 sections with 30,000 juveniles. As a. beneficiary order its funds stand in a very satisfactory position, and no one can estimate the amount of good done to the families of the members. Mr. Gledhill has offered a gold medal to the division making the largest increase in members. After the address there WSW an interesting discussion, in which the following took part; Messrs. Thomas Cooke, D. Bisbrown, Thomas R. Whyte, Thomas Clarke, Daniel Kirkham (Weston), and Will lain Hazlehurst (Weston.)

RUNCORN NOTES, [By A CORRESPONDENT.]

It is a matter for deep regret that the snail-pox scourge has broken out at Halton. It was hoped by everyone that the disease had been thoroughly stamped out, but unfortunately this is not so. The patients have been removed to the Moore Hospital and the necessary pre- cautions taken to prevent the disease from still further spreading.

It is gratifying to note that the output of books at the Runcorn Free Library continues steadily on the increase. Last month was a record since the establishnient of the institution. On the Saturday evening Mrs Jones was employed changing. books at the rate of two a minute. It is quite time now that the committee- considered the advisability of procuring an assistant. A word to the wise is sufficient.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTISALT..

By placing Widnes hors-de-combat, Runcorn have entered the second round of the Northern Union Cup Ties. The game was not by any means a good exposi- tion of football. But, for once, science overcame brute strength and the Chemicals were made to *mourn:. The largest crowd this season watched the match and, as there were fully two • thousand Widnes spectators, there was quite a jubilant howl when the spin of the coin gave the visitors the choice of ends. With a strong wind at their backs it was expected that the blue and white hoops would pile up a satisfactory. score. But this hope was doomed to bitter disappoint- ment. It is true that in the first half they attacked strongly, and the home contingent were kept on the qui rive defending their lines. The Widnes efforts, however, lacked finish, and the team lost several valuable opportunities by sheer waste of energy. The dropped goal was a fair indication of the game at half- time. It was a beautiful kick and Douglas was deserv- edly applauded.

Inthe second half the Linnets took up the running, and it was not long before Price equalised with a line goal. Matters slowed down considerably with both teams claiming equal points, and it seemed as though the game would end in a draw. After a lot of desultory play, Jolley and Richardson initiated several brilliant swoops on the thin white line, which at last resulted in the leather being grounded in the necessary place by Butterworth. The home contingent easily maintained the upper hand to the finish.

As all expected, the match was not a pleasing one from aspectacular point of view. The ball was kept close all the time and there was little beyond scrum, scrum, scrum, and kick, kick, kick. Penalties were fre- quent and, once :or twice a little bad blood was shown by the players. Perhaps the most conspicuous player for downright "cheek "—to use a mild word—was Aspey. If he does not alter his style of play he'll find himself suspended ere long. The defence of the visitors was far superior to their attacking powers, and the for- ward play much superior to the back. Runcorn played by far the better game and deserved to win. Had Jolley parted with the leather a little oftener there might have been a much heavier score. All the Runcorn lads worked hard.

The Examiner Friday, February 20, 1903.

WIDNES AND RUNCORN BRIDGE.

Progress of the Work.

Considerable progress has been made with the erec- tion of this important structure during the last few months, and the works are now assuming an interesting stage. The foundations are completed on both sides of the river, the eight large cast cylinders having been taken down to the solid rock, about 35ft. below the Manchester Ship Canal, and filled with strong Portland cement concrete. These cylinders are over 9ft. in diameter, and are braced together in clusters of four with strong steel bracing. In sinking these cylinders through the Ship Canal bed compressed air was adopted,the cylinders acting as cais- sons, which made the operation rather tedious on accOunt of the excavated material having to be passed through the air-lock. The foundation cylinders on the Widnes side were most easily laid, the rock being bare at low water. A large portion of this rock was rotten at the surface, and had to be removed to get to the solid rock, to which the cylinders were securely bolted. The erection of the four steel towers—each of which will be about 190 ft. high—will shortly commence' the erection being carried out simultaneously on the four clusters of cylinders on each side of the river. A tall braced steel "stallage." 140 ft. high, will be used for the purpose, having a derrick crane on the top. This stall- age must not be mistaken for the permanent towers of the bridge. The anchorages are completed on the Runcorn side, and nearly so on the Widnes side, the massive anchor plates being embedded in the solid rock about 30ft from the surface, and filled in with strong cement con- crete. The masonry approaches on the Runcorn side • which have necessitated the construct on of massive stone and concrete retaining walls, diversion of sewers railway siding, etc., are nearly completed, and will make a wonderful improvement to the appearance of that part of the town. All the stonework is ready for con- structing the approach on the Widnes side, and this will be nearly completed in about a month's time, including the subway under the approach, which will be lined throughout with white glazed bricks. The towers will probably be completely erected by the middle of April, when the important and interesting operation of laying the massive steel cables, each of which is over 12in. in diameter, will be commenced. The bridge will be totally unlike any other structure in England. On account of the almost unique condi- tions, and the great span of I,oooft.clear, the design and erection must naturally be a source of great anxiety to the engineers—Mr. John J. Webster M.lnst.C.E., of Westminster, and Mr. John 'l'. Wood, M.lnst.C.E., of Liverpool; but everything at present points to the successful completion of a work which will be of un- doubted benefit to the residents in the two adjacent counties. It is expected that a great wheel and foot traffic will be created by the bridge. Sir John Brunner Bart., M.P., is chairman of the company which is carry- ing out the scheme.

ALLEGED THEFTS AT EARLESTOWN. Prisoner Committed for Trial.

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Tuesday afternoon before Mr. F'. A. 'Frost, Daniel Jones, collier, a native of Llanwrst, near Mold,was charged with steal- ing a mutfler.value 3g. 6d. the property of George Barrow, Fairclough-street, Earlestown, between the 7th and 9th Inst.

Prosecutor, who is a blacksmith, stated that prisoner lodged with him. On Sunday, February Bth, witness missed the muffler named in the charge, which was safe in the cupboard the previous day. Subsequently he saw the muffler in the possession of P.C. Lennie. Frank Faulkner, pawnbroker's assistant in the employ of Mr.A. C. Fletcher, Junction-lane, St. Helens, stated that on the morning of February 10th prisoner came to his employer's shop and pledged themnifier for sixpence stating that he wanted the money to buy food. P. C. Lennie stated that on February 10th he appre- hended prisoner on another charge and found the pawn- ticket relating to the muffler in his possession. On being charged prisbner said "I did not steal it. I picked it off the floor."

Prisoner said he found the muffler on the floor under the table when he got up to make the fire. He put it in his pocket and no one made any complaint to him. He was committed for trial at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

Prisoner was then charged with stealing an overcoat and a pair of gloves value £2. the property of Joseph Austin, grocer, King Street, Earlestown. Prosecutor stated that on the sth. instant the articles 'were in the lobby at his house, and on the 7th. he missed them. Information was given to the police. P.C. Lennie stated that at 6.15 p.m. on the 10th inst. he was on duty in Back Market-street, narlestown, and saw the prisoner who was wearing the overcoat produced. Witness had received information of the theft from Mr. Austin's house, and questioned the prisoner, who stated that he bought the overcoat for ss. from a man in a public-house. Witness took him to the Police Station and on searching him found the gloves produced in one of the pockets of the overcoat. In answer to the charge prisoner again stated that he bought the coat in a public-house, but made a contra- dictory statement in regard to the wane of the house. Prisoner, who adhered to his statement that he bought the coat,was committed for trial on this charge also. There is a long list of previous convictions against him.

WARRINGTON DRAUGHTS LEAGUE.

WARRINGTON D. & C.C. v. CAIRO STREET D.C. The first match between these clubs in this season's League Competition, for the "Chalmers" Cup, was- played last Monday evening at Cairo-street schools, and resulted ma decisive victory for Warrington. Score as ollows • :- WiRRINGTON. C A IRO-STREEF. Wins. Draws. Wins.

The result of this Twitch virtually decides the destina- tion of the cup for this season, Warrington having won all their matches so far, and only two fixtures to play, while Cairo-street, having lost two matches, is com- paratively "out of the running." Tomkinson is to be congratulated in snatching a win from the Warrington Champion, the only game the latter has lost in these League matches for a number of years past. It will be observed that he and D. Plinston were the only players to score for Cairo-street. The Warrington Club is playing very strong this season, as the matches with other towns have shown.

Arrangements have been made for Mr. Jordan, the world's champion, to visit Warrington, under the auspices of the Warrington Draught League, on Monday next, the 23rd inst. Play wilt take place at Cairo-street schools from 7 to 10 p.m., and every draughts player in the town should take advantage of this opportunity to have a game with the champion, or they will miss a treat. Mr. Jordan is not particular as to the number of boards he keeps going at one time, the more the merrier; and it is acredit to any player to get a drawn game with him, even under such circumstances. Last week he played 600 games at Manchester, of which he lost only eight, and won over 500..

HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD v. BOWDEN COLLEGE.

Played at Latchford, on Saturday, a very fast and vigorous game resulting in a win for the visitors by two goals to one. Nothing was scored during the initial half, and the visitors were the first to break through from the restart, but 13anks soon equalised with a good shot after a neat dribble. Just before the close of the game Bowden scored a second goal. Hallows for Latchford, played a fine game at full back and Banks was the pick of the forwards.

WHOLESALE SLOT METER ROBBERIES AT WIDNES. Before Mr. Paton and Mr. Thomas at Widnes, on Thursday, John William Ogden, fourteen years of age, of James-street, and William Hampson, fourteen years of age, of Cholmondeley-street, were chaTged- with breaking and robbing several slot meters in the town during the last three weeks. Both pleaded guilty to the offences, they having divided the spoil. Ogden was implicated in fifteen and Hampson eleven robberies of like kind. The bench spoke strongly upon the influence which Ogden had brought to bear upon Hampson,and aftera severe reprimand they discharged him, and Ogden was sent to a reformatory for three years.

Games were indulged in until supper, and alto- gether a very pleasant evening was spent. EARLESTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

There was really nothing much to justify the as- sumption that Warrington would beat the Saints on Saturday. Lowly as the latter's position is, that of Warrington is little better and their recent form has been of the poorest description. The hope was really based on the fact that Warrington had a full team, with the exception of Fish, while as a rule they have been two or three men short for important engage- ments. A back division consisting of Hallam, Hock- enhall, Isherwood, Dickenson, Harris, Burgess and Duckworth we thought sufficiently capable to dismiss St. Helens, and so it would be under ordinary cir- cumstances, but the circumstances on Saturday were really extraordinary. I will explain—not excuse!

The remarkable part of the game was the way the home forwards simply outclassed the Warrington eight. After the.first quarter of an hour Briers went into the threequarter line, and the remaining seven played such n dashing, brilliant game that they com- pletely demoralised the Warrington team fore and, aft.

It is to the everlasting disgrace of the Warrington forwards that they allowed seven men to toy with them in the ridiculous fashion of last Saturday. The Warrington eight might have been collected from a mob of salmon-tin punchers, so devoid of both strength and skill were they. In all my experience —and it is now getting fairly lengthy—l never saw such an exhibition. Presumably the Warrington eight were triers, but with about two exceptions, Forster and Naylor' they did not give that impression. St. If,elens put double the amount of vim and deter- mination into their play, and this was the secret of their success. They played a thorough bustling cup- tie game, Whereas Warrington never seemed to get into their stride at all. The poor play of the forwards apparently infected the backs for the few chances that came their way were turned to no account.

The Warrington sp?ctators showed their loyalty to the club once more, and over a thousand of them journeyed to the pill-centre in order to see the cup- ties commence. Cup-tie fever is wonderfully stimu- lating, but it did not spread very far at St. Helens, for the whole crowd did not number more than about 3,000, and the gross receipts were only z(--;71. War- rington's share will therefore just about pay expenses.

The 169th quarterly meeting of this society, was held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestotvn on Monday even- ing. Mr. D. Forrester presided, but there was only a moderate attendance of members. The report and statement of accounts showed that the cash receipts were £12,918 195.53 d. being an increase over last quarter of £7 10s. 93d., and on the corresponding quarter of £Bl7 ss. 53. The net profits realised were £1,831 175., which with £BO 4s. sd. from the dividend reserve fund, wonld enable the committee to declare a dividend of 2s. 9d. in the to members. During the quarter 116 new members had been admitted, and 36 had withdrawn, leaving 1,950 on the register of whom 1,560 had brought in checks. Mr. D. Forrester was re-elected president for another 12 months' and Mr. T. Haselden, auditor. Messrs. Worsley and Owen were appointed members delegates to the C. W. S. quarterly meeting. The com- mittee were given authority to organise another Child- dren's field thy, on the same lines as last year. A grant of £5 was made to the local lecture fund, and £5 ss. to the Southport Convalescent Home. SID BURNING FATALITY AT HAYDOCK.

Owing to a wait of a quarter of an hour for Burgess, the game did not start until 3-45, but the opening stages were sufficiently sensational to make Up for the delay. St. Helens, with the advantage of a strong wind at once attacked, but were quickly repelled by the Warrington backs, Dickenson showing a glimpse of form in an effort which carried play into the St. Helens half. Warrington got the ball again, and Duckworth after a sho:t run threw the ball over to Forster, who took it dearly and showing some of his old threequarter form raced through the St. Helens backs and notched an unmistakably fine try at the corner. Though Hallam failed at goal, you could, at this stage have offered almost any odds on War- rington and they would not have been accepted. To score against the wind appeared to make the match a certainty for them, and if the St. Helens players had been as disheartened as their spectators the result probably would have been very different. But they were not beaten by any means, and for the rest of the forty minutes they fairly bombarded the Warrington line, but were kept out by grand tackling. The Saints then showed a resourcefulness which won the match. Banks dropped a goal, then Pennington placed one from a penalty, and to crown all Foulkes dropped another with a beautifully judged shot. Warring- ton's score was thus doubled by the Saints in a fashion which it was difficult to prevent.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquiry at Blackbrook, Haydock, on •S'aturday, touching the death of Elizabeth Ann Marsh, aged 39, of 14, Park-street, Haydock, who died on Thursday under exceedingly sad circumstances. John Marsh, husband of the deceased, stated that his wife had been ill for a week and con- fined to bed. Her eyes became very much inflamed, and for three days she could not see. He understood that, she had acute neuralgia in her eyes and head. On Wed- nesday he sat up until midnight and fell fast asleep. There was a lire in the room and the gas was lighted. About one o'clock in the morning he was awakened,and found the curtains and bed clothes on tire. He jumped up and his foot caught against the deceased, who was lying on the floor with her night clothes on lire. He put the flames out with a shawl. tor. Dowling was called in, and he said she was very seriously burnt.— Mrs. Charlson, the deceased's mother, said her daughter was very weak. She told her she had fallen into the fire. Witness thought that when deceased got' out of lied her extreme weakness had caused her to faint, and she consequently got on fire. The jury returned a ver- dict of" Accidental death."

Still, with only three points to wipe off, Warring- ton appeared to have an easy task on turning round. The wind was as strong as ever, but some time. elapsed before Warrington gained any advantage. Their supporters began to feel somewhat uneasy, but they were still sure of success. At last Warrington got near the line----the doom of the-homesters was sealed! Instead they got the hall and by keeping, continually in possession of it they worked Warring- ton back to safer quarters. Two penalties were given, which should - have won the match but Hallam and .Burgess. showed a lamentable inability to follow the example of the Saints. Matters soon began to get desperate. It became a question whether Warrington -would get the try which would make • a draw. Time passed and it became clear that the visitors were getting beaten. St. Helens would not part with the ball for a moment and the rejuvenated -Pennington spent half his time in hugging it close to the ground. The Warrington backs—in fact the whole team—was simply impotent, and barring an effort by Harris,never looked like scoring the -winning point. St. Helens outplayed and outstayed them, and quite deservedly won the match.

The game was very uninteresting, though there was plenty of excitement when either side got near the line. The only thing I can compliment Warrington on is their defence in the first half. Individually not a man played up to his best form, with the aforesaid exception of Forster and Naylor.

The Lancashire clubs suffered in the first round, for several of the best were drawn together, with the result that, in addition to Warrington, the following bit the dust and said good-bye to the cup for another season: Broughton Rangers (the holders), Widnes, and Swinton, the latter of whom were defeated by Leigh in the replay at Swinton on Wednesday.

The draw for the second round shows at once how advantageous the new system is for the Yorkshire clubs. The Lancashire first Leaguers, with the ex- ception of St. Helens and Runcorn, are all drawn against one another, while the Tykes have almost all got "soft things " on with the numerous small clubs left in the broad-acred shire. In fact not one York- shire .Senior League club is drawn against another. Poor Lancashire will have precious few representa- tives of any calibre to uphold the honour of the red rose in round three.

For the second round the draw is as fol1v: Outwood Church v. Hunslet. Morecambe v. Minom St. Helens v. Rochdale Hornets. Keighley v. Manningham, York v. Otley or Ossett. Oldham v. Wigan. Normanton v. Batley. Bromley v. Bradford. Castleford v. Halifax. Runcorn v. Birkenhead. Hull Marlborough v. Hull. Brighouse Rangers v. South Shields. Wakefield Trinity v. Leeds. Salford v. Leigh. ,Maryport v. Parton. Wath Brow v. Barrow.

GILCHRIST LECTURE AT EARLESTOWN.

"Weather and Weather Forecasting,"

The third lecture of the course of five under the auspices oil he Newton-in-Makerlield Lectures Committee was delivered in the Town Hall, Rarlestown, on Monday evening by Mr. Hugh Robert Mill the director of the British Rainfall Organisation and secretary of the Royal • Meteorological Society, his subject being .".Weather and Weather Forecasting." As usual the hall was packed, the Chair being occupied by Mr. Arthur Smith. In introducing the lecturer the Chairman said that the subject of the lecture was a most interesting and fascinating one; the English clinrite was so mysterious in its working. The lecturer opened by remarking that it was a strik ing fact that whenever the English people were gathered together the first observations they make are associated with the weather. The changes of the weather are so frequent that those people who venture to predict certain changes run a great deal of risk. The state of the weather depended on the atmosphere at any time with regard to wind, warmth, wetness and electrical condition. Any increase of pressure in the air increases the temperature. .If the air from a bicycle tyro was allowed to escape, the valve imme- diately became cold. Air is a mixture of invisible gases one of which—water vapour—becomes as cloud, mist, rain, dew, snow, hail, or hoar frost. The highest clouds are composed of little crystals of ice. There is a general circulation of air over the sur- face of the globe in the course of which whirls are set up like eddies in a stream of water. There are two dis- tinct forms of air-whirl each associated with a par- ticular distribution of atmospheric pressure. There are the inward and upward whirl and tte downward and outward whirl. An inward whirl of air towards a cen- tre of low pressure where the incoming air escapes up- wards is called a depression or cyclone. These are ex- perienced in their worst forms in Eastern countries, and are called Simons. In the Sahara a cyclone sometimes causes sand to be carried thousands of miles by the wind and cases are on record where sand has been blown from this desert and dropped in England. A- nother form of cyclone is the American tornado which also .works great havoc. The rising air cools by expansion and forms clouds whence rain falls. The wind goes from a centre and expands. As it expands it becomes a cloud and the eloud falls in the form of rain or hail or snow. Tf they understood these two whirls, the lecturer informed his audience, they would understand more about the weather than most people who have studied it for a long time. The lecturer afterwards displayed a chart, which he and his predecessor had compiled, denoting the various annual rainfall in the British Isles. This showed that the wettest year we have experienced was 1872 and the driest.lBB7.The 30th December 1900 was the wettestday he rver remembered. A curious fact gathered from the chart ,v;ls that latterly, two dry years had been succeeded by. an exceptionally wet One. As 1901,and 1902 were dry years if the same ride obtained, we should experience a, wet year in 1903. It may be in 20 years, it may be next year, but it was pretty certain that sometime we should enter on another long period of wet weather. February 11891 was the driest month 'ever recorded, there being I no rain at all during the month.

The lecture was illustrated by pictures from the oxyhy- drogen lantern.

Mr. J. IL Profit •'proposed a vote of thanks to the lecturer and Mr. F. Goslineconded, Mr. Mill respond- ing. Mr. Mill afterwards proposed a vote of thanks to the Chainnzip, who suitably responded.
