INFLUENZA INCREASING.

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the Country. The visitation is of .a very prostrating nature, and the dise.oe is certainly on the increase. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as mucha mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost ay other disease, and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who 'imagines he has only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It can hardly be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful influence of Influenza, whose after-effects so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fell., as a great many have not yet, if they ever will,regain their former health. As a preventative, we know of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often provedeffectual in ward- ing off attacks of this Malady, and always succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient Is under their Influence. Do you suffer from Chills, alternating witty Sudden Flushes. Severe Head-ache, accompanied by difficulty of breathing, and symptoms of Catarrh, Pains in the Chest, under and between the shoulders, and a stiffness and soreness of the Muscles, Loss of Appetite and Nervousness. If you suffer from any of these symp- toms, know that they are the forerunners of Influenza, and it behoves you to resort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By common consent Patients everywhere admit that they feel more depressed and miser- able after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of any other illness. The best way to banish these miser- able feelings is to take Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, [he egetable Tonic. We can strongly and confident re recommend this preparation, as it has treated seve cases of Influenza, Heavy Colds, Extreme Weakness' end Langour, with greater success than any other known Remedy. We may also add that as a Remedy for Indi- gestion, Sleeplessness, Low Spirits, Nervousness. and Loss of Appetite, Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters stands unequalled. CRUELTY TO HORSES.-WARRINGTON,. MEN FINED,

_ Two R.S.P.C.A. prosecutions occupied the attention of the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday. ln the first case, James Kilduff, 24, Marsh- street, was charged with cruelty to a horse, by working it whilst it was in an unfit state.on 7th February.— Inspector Print stated that he saw a boy in, charge of a b horse belongino, to the defendant., whicwhichwasunfit for work, but as the lad was only thirteen years of age he did not summon him. Subsequently he saw the defen- dant:and questioned him as to the sores on the horse's back. Defendant stated that he knew the horse had some heat lumps. The saddle was pressing on one of the sores, and blood and matter were adhering to it.— Defendant's excuse was that the sores had been caused through kindness. The food which he gave the animal was of such a good quality as to cause the heat lumps. —A fine of ss. was imposed.—Harry Acton, 48, Birchall-street, and Thomas Donoghue, 23, Lloyd- street, were charged with ill-treating a horse the first-named by beating it, and Donoghue by spurring it on February lst.—P.C. Lewis stated that he was on duty near St. Ann's Church when the defendants passed him in charge of a brown horse. They were coming in the direction of Winwick. Donoghue was leading the horse by the bridle and Acton was walking behind. Shortly after passing witness. Acton commenced to beat the horse very peverely with the heavy end .of a whip- stock. The blows could be heard 200 yards away. At Longford Bridge, Donoghne mounted the animal and spurred it, Acton continuing to beat it with the whip- stock. Witness followed the defendants and questioned them, and Donoghue said the horse was stupid and he could do no good with it. The horse was bleeding where the spurs had been applied, and there were lumps caused by the beating.—Owen Toole, Inspector Print, and Ser- geant Evans, having given evidence, defendants were each fined ss. and costs.

CAUGHT IN THE ACT.-THEFT OF CHEESE FROM A WINWICK SHOP..

At the WarringtonveOn.nt-YWiti-Sessions on Wednes- day, before Mr. J. J. Bleckly and other magistrates, Frederick Lomax (21) and Robert Blair (15), were charged with stealing a pound of cheese, of the value of tl/41. from the shop of Ellen Porter, grocer, Winwick, on Saturday.—The evidence showed that prisoners went into the shop and asked for a pennyworth of bread. Prosecutor's daughter, who was in the shop at the time, went towards the kitchen to ask her mother to serve them. Whilst her back was turned, 'lrs. Woof, wife of P.C. Woof, who resides next door, saw Lomax go round the corner and take something and put it under his coat. She raised the alarm and prosecutor's daughter came back into the shop and got hold of Lomax, who threw a pound of cheese which he had taken across the shop. Prisoners were taken into custody by P.C. Woof and on being charged stated that they went to the shop for a pennyworth of bread. .The only money they had was a penny and they took the cheese under the impression that it was butter.—Pri- soners are natives of Liverpool.—The magistrates bound Blair overto come up for judgment when calleo.upon and imposed a line of 20s. and costs, with the option of 14 days imprisonment, upon Lomax.

Warrington County Petty Sessions.

WEDNESDAY.—Before _Messrs: J. .T. Bleckley (in tke

chair), J. Ball mid S. R. Armitage. USW E I 011 ED BILE A D.—James Knowles, Sankey ; Betsy Horridge, Poulton; and William Booth, Poulton, were each fined Is. and costs, for selling . bread other- wise than by weight, at their various premises.—Mr. Richard Evans, solicitor, appeared for Mr. Knowles and -said there was no intention to defraud. The defendant was busy attending to another customer when the loaf was sold, and he forgot to weigh it. He had been in business for fifteen years and had never been stuaunoned for anything of the kind before.

THE BARTON LOCK SMASH.

legel Rackless Navigation.

Azti3il in the Admiralty Coart.

In the Admiralty Division of the High Court of Jus- tice on Wednesday, the case of the Manchester Ship Canal Company v. the owners of the steamship Wink- field came on for hearing before Mr. Justice Bucknill, sitting with Trinity Masters. Mr. Pickford, K.C., Mr. B. Aspinall, K.C., and Mr. Lewis Noad (instructed by Messrs. Hill, Dickinson,and Co. solicitors, Liverpool), appeared for the plaintiffs; and Mr. T. M. Scrutton, K.C., and Mr. D. Stephens for the defendants. _

This was an action for damages to the -Barton lock, Which the plaintiffs said h‘ad been occasioned by the defendanus' vessel having come into collision with the lock gates. Mr. J. W. Lane, who was in charge of the lock at the time—December 10 list—said the steamship Winkfield was so negligently navigated that she collided with and carried away the inner lock gates, and sank a tug. The Winkfleld, 4,116 tons gross, was bound_ for Manchester to Runcorn, with part cargo for Brunswick, B.N.A. Thu case for the defend tnts is that-what occurred was an "inevitable ‘accident," due to the telegraphing apparatus breaking down, and that in these circum- stances they are not liable for damages. The evidence, which was chiefly technical, among the witnesses being Sir Fortescue Flannery, M.P., had not been concluded when the court rose for the day. _

His Lordship, in giving judgment on Thursaay said that both he and the Trinity Masters had come to the conclusion from the evidence that the defendants must be held to blame. In the view of the Court the collision was brought about by the negligent navigation of the Winkfield. The Court also considered that thetelegraph on board the vessel should be periodically inspected. That, in the opinion of the Elder Brethren, and he con- curred, would only be a right and proper thing to do. Judgment was accordingly entered for the plaintiffs, with a reference to the Registrar and merchants to assess the amount of the damVps.

Major Wilkinson, of Orf oil Barracks, was thrown from his . horse while out with the North Cheshire Hounds, on Saturday, and was seriously injured, the animal having stepped into a rabbit hole. The Major WAS picked up in a semi;couscions condition, placed in a trap, and driven to Mnrbury Hall.. Later on in the day he was brought to Warringtoa, and he is improv- ingTapidly. • Swing Boat Accident. Florence Noton, agel 11 years, who lives with her parents in Elizabeth-street met with a serious accid- enton Saturday evening. She was pres nt at the fair which has occupied the waste ground near Thewlis- street, Bank Quay, for some time, when a gust of wind blow off her hat. She ran after it, and when stooping..to pick it up, one of the swingboats struck her a terrific blow on the head, rpulering her uncon- scious. Pym Vicar of Thelwall, on Monday evening are some- what pculiar. Mr. Williamson was cycling to town along Knutsford-road on Monday evening. When near Black Bear bridg3 an electric car came up. and he endeavoured to give a bag to the conductor to take down. In doing so he was mught between the car and an electric trolley wire standard, and thrown off his machine, his left arm being broken. WARRINGTON EDUCATION SCFNIP

T3WII. COUNCIL APPOINTS THE COIAMITTEE.

Religious Rt3prasantatioa TEATERS AND TRADES COUN3IL TRIUMPH.

A special meeting of the Warrington Town Council was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening, the Mayor .in the chair, "for the purpose of making a scheme in pursuance of Section 17 of the Education Act, 1902, to establish and constitute an Education Com- mittee for the borough, and to order the common seal to be affixed thereto." There were also present : The Deputy-Mayor (Alderman J. Fairelough); Alder- men W. Bolton. j. S. Green, T. Hutehinson, F. W. Monks, and T. H. Sutton: Councillors T. Barton, A. Bennett, W. Brough, J. Brophy, T. Burton, J. G. Craik, S. Davies, D. S. Duncan, James Evans, John Evans, L. Ellison, W. J. Forshaw, T. J. Hulse, J. Lord, A: Pea- cock, J. Pemberton, W. Pierpoint, J. Roberts, F. R. Roberts, Win. Roberts, W. Rogers, J. Sampson, D. Tinnion, J. Upson, J. Wilkinson, with Messrs. J. L. Whittle (Town Clerk), S. Wilson (Deputy Town Clerk). J. Fairhurst (Borough Treasurer), and Mr. Murray, the Director of Education. There was a small attendance of the general public, including the Revs. John Yonge, R. Wilkins Rees, Messrs. E. Wallington, A. Corney, J. Stansfield, J. Parkinson: P. T. Winskill, Dunn, F. Pendlebury, and F. Stott.

The MAYOR said he need hardly tell the members that the business before them. was the most important that they had had to transact for a long time, and he trusted they would do their utmost to workicarefully, conscientiously and efficiently, and purely from an educa- tional point of view, without allowing any side issues to bias their judgment. The Museum Committee and the Town Clerk had drawn up a scheme with care and attention, and he trusted the members would see their way to deal with the matter in such a manner that they might formulate a really good workible system for the education of the rising generation of the town. (Hear, hear.) _ _ _

===== 0002601_19030220_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
PARAGRAPH BY PARAGRAPH. THE MATTER BEING RUSHED.

Councillor TENSION proposed that the Council discuss the scheme paragraph by paragraph. Councillor JAM ES EfANs seconded, and said that, considering the Council had a scheme before them that had been altered by the Board of Education since the draft was sent out, he considered it short notice to dis- cuss such a subject of great importance. He would prefer that the matter should be referred back to the General Purposes Committee in order that it might have the matured judgment not only of the Council but of those outside who were taking a very keen and deep int;.Test in education. He thought the -latter was being rushed.

The 2,1 A-Yon observed that :the Town Clerk had informed him -that they were mostly verbal alterations. Councillor EVAN'S: I have not had an opportunity of looking over them. Alderman Surros said the proper coarse was to hear what Alderman Monks had to say.

The resolution of Councillor tINNION was carried. ALDERIVIAN MONKS INTRODUCES THE .SCHEME.

Alderman MONKS said he had intended to suggest that the most effective way was to discuss the question paragraph by paragraph. He aid notknow that it would advance matters if he made a lengthy statement. He had given the matter great consideration, and the scheme was so complete and so self-explanatory that it. would be a waste of time to try to explain it that evening. He confirmed what the Ala.or had said as to the desire of the Museum Committee to submit such a scheme as would, in the opinion of the members of the eommittee, cover all the educational requirements of the borough, and be likely to prove a success. There was no wish to rush the matter, which could be adjoitrned for a month if the Council desired. There were, however, reasons why the scheme should be adopted and put into opera- tion at the earliest possible day. It was too important to be left over altogether, as the necessary arrangements for the classes had to be made without delay. He suggested that clause 3 should be taken first. Thealtera- tions of the Board of Education were not important; they were merely another way of stating what the Museum Committee recommended. CORRESPONDENCE. APPEALS FROM LOCAL BODIES.

The TOWN CLERK said he had received a long letter from the Trades Lind Labour Council, a copy of whieh had been sent to each member of the Council. The letter read as follows :- 1, Leonard-street, Warrington. February 16th, 1903. Dear Sir,—We regret that no provision has been made for a representative of our council being elected to the proposed Education Committee, and are taking this opportunity of placing a few facts before you in the hope that at the meeting of the Town Council to- morrow you will support our claim to nominate one member of the committee. Such provision has been made in many other towns, and we claim to be the most eligible of any working-class organization in Warring- ton for a seat on the encationconunittee. "The Trades Council directly represents, and is responsible for, more than 2,000 organized skilled artisans, comprising the principal trades unions. We can also claim tnrepre- sent (although in an indirect way) the whole of the working classes in Warrington. Our aims and objects are to improve the conditions of life of the workers as a whole, unionists and non-unionists, and also any measure of reform that we advocate and agitate for has that end in view. The work of friendly socteties,though excellent in itself,is necessarilyilimited to the providing of assistance to its members only,during periods of sickness and infirmity, nor can we say that friendly sobieties are entirely working-class org mizations, whereas the Trades Council deals with any or all questions affect7ng the workers, and is purely a working-class organization. The Working Men's Infirmary Committee (which is being recommended for the distinction) can in no sense ho said to represent the workera. of Warrington. The duties of the committee are to collect and transmit contributions to the Infirmary exchequer. The members of the committee are mainly foramen and minagers, and are not elected by votes of the employes of the firms they claim to represent. The ny3mbers of the Trades Council are elected by the direct vote of organized workers. The Chamber of Commerce recognized our Council as the representative organization of the work- ing classes, which fact of itself should be sufficient guarantee for us being allowed the same privilege as they are being granted. We would also point out that practically the whole of the trades unionists are mem- bers of friendly societies though All members of friendly societies are not menibers of trades uniens. Therefore by inviting our Council to elect a delegate the whole of the friendly societies would also be represented. Resolutions have been passed in .support of .our claims by a large number of trades union branches, some of which are not members of our Coun- cil. We are confident, that had there been sufficient time, every trades union branch, and many branches of friendly societies also, would have passed similarreso- lutions in our favour. What we desire is, that a trades unionist, nominated by the Trades and Labour Council, shall be accepted on the Education Committee.—Hop- ing that you will give your support on our behalf, we remain. yours faithfully, JOHNEDWARDSON (president), FRED Si.OTT (secretary)." The Tows CLERK 'added that the following associations had sent letters in support of the Trades and Labour Council :

Amalpmated Society of Mill Sawyers, Woodcutting Machinists and Wood-turners. Independant Labour Party. National Amalgamated Union of Enginemen, Crane- men, Fltunmer, Steam and Electric Tram-drivers, and Boiler Firemen.

Wheelwrights' and Smiths' Society. Amalgamated Union of Operative Bakers and Con- fectioners. National Union of Gas Workers and General Labour- ers.

Nationfil AmaNl:Tilted Society of Operative House and Ship Painters and Decorators.

The Amalgamated Wiredrawers' Society of Great Britain. Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants. Amalgamated Union of Shop Assistants, Warehouse- men and Clerks. Braziers and Sheet Metal Workers' Society.

The following letter was-read from the 'Warrington Building -industries Federation :—At the last inee4iohg of the above federation a resolution was passed, which I was instructed to for.vard to you, requesting you to lay the same before the .special meeting of the town Council. " Thatthis federation is unanimously of o- pinion that the trade societies of Warrington ought to have a representative on the new education autliprity, and that this federation, together with* the Trades-Coun- cil, ought to have the privilege of nominating the rep-. resentative.—YOurs respectfully... 0. BTTCKLKY.

The SEcRETARY to the Friendly Societies' 'Council wrote notice by raports in the local papers that the Town Council are about to appoint a committee to carry out the new Education Act, and in addition to the members of the Council it is proposed to add one or more representatives trom local bodies. I respectfully claim that our Council ought not to be overlooked, and at the least ought to have one or more representatives upon the committee. The Friendly Societies' Council is now the recognized central bodyof the affiliated socie- ties in the town, and numbers over 7,000 members, and prior to the abolition of school fees all our societies took an active part in the education of our deceased members' children, and front the widows' and orphans' funds paid the school pence, amounting in the aggregate to hundreds of pounds, and now their wants are periodic- ally inquired into and assistance given. Thus it will be seen that the edu,aation of our me nbers' children has always occupied the attention of our odicials, and wore we to be favoured with a representative on the com- mittee immediate steps would be taken to appoint one.

Trusting this \yid have your earn3st consideration. Yours, &c., Richard E. Pooley.

The7Tov:rN CuEnK said he' had also received a letter from the Private Schools' Association (Incorporated) Liverpool Branch. The letter,dated from 23,WilsonPatten-street, read:— "As I reside in Warrington,l have been requested by the Council of the Private Schools' Association to make the following inquiries Will the Director of Educa- tion have any control overprivate schools in Warring- ton? If so, what form will it take? 2. When the local Education Committee be formed, will private schools be represented? 3. if representation be accorded to private schools, will the representative be selected by the Council or by the private schools.— Yours obediently, KA.TE RIAINI OR. hon treasurer."

THE CO-OPTED REPRESENTATIVES. THE RELIGIOUS DIFFICULTY ARISES AND SUBSIDES.

Alderman SELAW GREEN said it seemed to him that paragraph 1. contained the crux of the whole question. He thought the number of members suggested WaS alto- gether too large. A MEMRER: You will have to :make it larger. Alderman SAW GREEN said 1S members would be ample, twelve of the Council and six from outside, for the purpose of .carrying the Act into effect. Alderman BOLTON said he was in favour of there being two members of the Council on the Committee to every outsider. The Council would be blamed for the expenditure of the money, and they 'ought to have a majority. (Hear, hear.) It was decided to first consider C 131133 3 of the scheme as amended by the Board of Education, and which read as follows : The co-optative members shall be com- petent persons of experience in education or acquainted with the needs of the various kinds of schools in the borough, and shall be appointed after consideration by ,the Council of 'recommendations from the following bodies in the following proportions that is to say:— One by an association of Church of England Elementary Schools within the Borough, recognized by the Council for the purpose; one by an association of Roman Catholic Elementary schools within the borough, recog- nized by the Council for the purpose; one by the Governors of the Warrington Grammar School; one (a woman) by the Council of the Warrington Diocesan Training College ; one by the Warrington Chamber of Commerce; one by the Workingmen's Committee of the Warrington Infirmary; three by the Education Committee. The persons to be recommended by the Education Committee must be persons experienced in Secondary and:Higher Education. In the event of any of the recommending bodies ceasing to exist, the Conn- , cil shall substitute such other body as in their opinion is of the same character or represents the same interests as the body which has ceased to exist." Councillor TINSCON said he had an objection to lump- ing together the Nonconformist and tha Unsettarian schools. Rather than agree to the curtailing of the committee, as suggested by Alderman Shaw Green, he was hnlined to in.:rease the number of outsiders and also the representatives of the Council. He proposed as an amendment that the Nonconformist Elementary schools and that the Unsectarian schools each have a representative. He did not object to the Grammar School, the Training College, or the Chamber of Com- merce each having a member on the committee, but he was in a serious diliculty in regard to the Working Men's Committee of the Infirmary having a representa- tive. (Hear, heir.) If that organization was to have one he contended that the Friendly Societies and the Trades and Labour Ceuncil,were entitled to one, and he thought the Teachers' Association should have one also. (Hear, hear.) %Viten he read the paragraph which in- ferred that no schoolmasters need apply he was vary much struck. It was evidently carefully hidden. He did not agree that schoolmasters should have no place on the committee and that it was entirely outside their sphere. They were entitled to more consider ation than theyhad been shown byjthe committee which had drawn up the scheme. They wanted the committee to be repre- sentative of every section. He did not see why the com- mittee should not be a large one, and with that object in view he would endeavour to enlarge the number of co-optative members on the lines he had mentioned.

- Tha MAYOR observed that it had 1,;(;;Cs-u—iies—CaCiThat each paragraph in the clause should be taken separately. Alderman MONICS then proposed, and Councillor BENNETT seconded, that the first paragraph relating to the Church of England schools be agreed to. Alderman HUTCIIINSON contended that three-fourths or at least one-half of the children in the town were edacated in church schools, and he thought the repre- sentation on the committee would be more equally divided if the church schools were allowed two repre- sentatives instead of one as proposed. He proposed that the Church schools have two representatives. Councillor Camic seconded the amendment: He con- sidered the representation proposed ifttogether inade- quate for the number of Church schools that would be taken over. The school buildings connected with the Parish Church were worth £30,003. Councillor F. R. RosErus said the Church wauld also be represented by the nominee of the governors of the Grammar School—(" No, no.") The Grammar School was a Church of England foundation, as Was also the Diocesan Training College. They might take it that. the representatives of these' institutions would be mem- bers of the Church of England. At present the Noncon- formists had only half a representative. (Laughter.) If they gave the Nonconformists a member, he was dis- posed to vote for two representatives of the Church schools.

Councillor BENNETT did not see any necessity for drawing any distinction between the Nonconformist and Unsectarian schools. For all practical purposes the terms were synonymous. Councillor IT. ULSE said the question of representation was discussed at a meeting of managers of Church of England schools that afternoon. They were quite satisfied with one representative', lent he should ask for two if the Nonconformists and Unsectarian schools were to have one each.

COunciiiir WILLIAM ROBF.R.TS thou,ght it would be a reasonable mpromise if the Nonconformists were allowed to ndllinate one representative, the linsectaria,n school in tnagera one representative, and the Church of England two representatives.

Coundillor -TiNXION said as a compromise he would accept that. _ . _

Councillor BENNETT protested, and said if they began to multiply the co-optative members they woiAld have a balky committee which would be practically unworkable. If every association were to be represented and they (the Corporation) appointed a member of the Council pro rata,—they would have to get a larger Council. Councillor TINsioN: You are dreaming again. (Laughter.) Councillor BENNETT said he was only trying to show that if they went on in that telescopic manner they would have an unworkable committee. He thought one representative for the Church of England, schools and one for the Nonconformist and Unsnctarian Salleels was sufficient.

Councillor JAMES 'EVANS said the Nonconformists were airciou4 to have a rapre3eotative of their own. With reference to the Training College representative, he believed that 95 per cent, of the persons in that college were not Warrington citizens. Councillor HuLsii:: That does not matter. Councillor JAMEi EVANS: - I think it matters a groat deal, especially when you are considering the basis of representation. Then with regard to the Grammar School, about -15 per cent, of the boys attending there were not actually Warrington boys, and their parents did not pay Borough rates. He claimed that the British Schools and the Nonconformist Schools had a stronger claim than the Grammar School or the Training Col- lege. (Hear, hear). It was right to give the Church of Eqgland schools a fair representation. Councillor PIERPOINer said he had no objection to the Council settling the religious question in the way they thought best, but he was afraid if they enlarged the committee in the manner proposed the Friendly Soci- eties, the Trades and Labour Council and the Teachers' Association would be left out in the cold. Councillor EVANS : No. Councillor PIERPOINT (continuing) said Alderman Shaw Green had spoken of a small committee. He (Councillor Pierpoint) thought it would be better to have a_ large Education Committee. Alderman MONKS said it -seemed to be altogether overlooked that in appointing representatives from the Grammar School and the Training College,they desired to obtain theservices of persons who knewsomething of the educational needs of the town and who would help the members of the Council in carrying out the re- quirements of the Act. He was sorry the matter had been introduced in the form it had., The educational needs of the town seemed to have been left out of the question altogether, and it seemed that individual repre- sentation was aimed at, and not the general interests of the children. He could not help thinking it was a mis- take not to accept the recommendation of the com- mittee so far as those were concerned. The amendment that the Church schools should have two representatives, the Roman Catholics one, the Non- conforfnists one, .and the Unsectarian schools one, was carried.

COUNCiLLOR, FORSHAW SUGGESTS AN ALTERAT[QN.

COAI,3IITTEE.

Councillor TINS/ON proposed that the Council grant the warrington Branch of the National Union of Teachers' the right of makin,, one nomination. Councillor DAVIES seconded.

Couacillo r F. R. FOBERTS said the object of the • whole Act was to have the education of the town carried on by a body representing all sections, and it would be little short of absurd if that portion of the constitu- ency best able to help them in the administration of the Act was shutout altogether. (Hear, hear.) They would want a good many hints and suggestions from those in the teaching profession, and in his opinion they - would be shutting the door against probably Via most useful representatives it would be possible o find if they excluded a representative of the National Union of Teachers. (Hear, hear). Councillor UPSON said members of the Council seemed to be fighting his battle so splendidly that it was hardly necessary for him to say much. The draft scheme which was submitted almost implied that "no schoolmaster need apply." If he ware asked to give an opinion upon the manufacture of \iron, the baking of bread or the brewing of beer it would carry no weight, and yet " Dick," " Tom "and "Harry " were supposed to be necessarily educational experts. (Laughter.) The teacher's position on the Committee was absolutely a legal one. It was expressly stated in the Act that a teacher was qualified to sit on the Committee, and therefore it was not a question of legality, but whether it was a proper thing to do or not. Councillor WILLIAM ROBERTS: The morality of the question. (Laughter.) Councillor UPsoN said he was quite disposed to dis- cuss it in that light. Without attempting to disparage those gentlemen who would be appointed by the Council to sit on the committee, it must be confessed that they had not that intimate knowledge of educational matters that a teacher daily engaged in school work had. (Hear, hear). It was said the committee would be the paymasters of such teachers,but that wasa feeble objec- tionetnd he wished to point out that there would be more money received in grants from the National Exchequer that would pay the salaries of all the teachers of the schools in Warrington. Therefore the objection was a slight one. It was of immense advantage to a com- mittee to have sitting on it an expert. He mentioned the Sanitary Works Committee for instance. All the members knew that it was immensely helpful in the committee to have sitting upon it such menus Council- lor John Evans and Councillor Dancan—(Hear, hear)— and a teacher would be in the same position on the Education Committee, as he came into touch with his professional brethren, and knew the needs of education better than anyone else. - (Hear, hear.) He had before him the education schemes of every County Council, Borough Council and Non-Borough Council that had been approved, and he found that more than four-fifths of these Councils had nominated an elementary teacher on the committee. (Hear, hear.) The County. Connell of Hertfordshire of which Lord Salisbury was a member had nominated two masters of experience Ca elementary education on the education committee, and what was good for Hertfordshire would be good for Warrington. He had also obtained particu- lars of the election of teachers on the educational authorities of France, Switerzland and Germany, and no member of the Council would pretend that we had come within sight of the educational efficiency of con- tinental countries in the matter of education. He found that in all continental countries, elementary teachers were allowed to take a part on,and werelable to render very efficient help to tho education committees. Councillor Upson concluded by reading an appreciation of the work of teachers on el:nation c3nrnittees by Mr. Horsfall.

Aldermn MONKS Said that if they gave representa- tion to the teachers they would next have an applica- tion from the headmasters of elementary schools for a representative. They had an association just as well. Councillor TINN EON : The National Union of Teachers includes the headmasters. Alderman Max}cs : But there is a Headmasters' Association.

Councillor Uesolst : Not in Warrington. Alderman MONKS : And we shall also have an appli- cation from, the teachers at the secondary schools, and where are we to draw the line ? If we give represen- tation to the teachers at all it should be given to head- masters as being men who have had a large experience and who would be more competent to help the com- mittee. We have a large number of teachers in War- rington who are not certified, who have not obtained the necessary scholarships established by the Govern- ment, and yet they claim to be represented on the Education Committee.

Councillor UPSON : The Warrington Teachers' Asso- ciation consists only of trained certificated teachers. Councillor TINNION : That is the association we are dealing with. Alderman MONKS: That I did not know. We have this claim for representation but do not know who com- pose the Association, and no information is given about it. Continuing he said quite recently an attempt was made to provide a special course of instruction for teachers. The scheme was taken up heartily in Widnes, Runcorn and St. Helens, and yet there were not more than six in Warrington who came forward, showing how little interest existed amongst the teachers in the matter of improving their qualifications and position. If a teacher were nominated it would be necessary to give him leave of absence to attend the meetings of the Education Committee, because they would have to be held in the day-time.

Councillor PEMBERTON referring to the moral ques- tion which had been introduced, said the teachers would only draw their salaries. They would not sell goods of a doubtful description to the Corporation. Councillor LORD: Will they float on companies? (Laughter.) _

• Councillor PEMBERTON (contihuing) said it was necessary to have persons on the committee .experi- enced in educational matters. H 3 was in favour of the Natioml .Union of Teachers nominating a representa- tive.

Councillor UPSON replied that it was not their fault that the teachers were partially qualified. It was be- cause the managers had had such limited means that. they had been compelled to employ partially qualified teachers. That should not be urged as a reflection on the Warrington Teachers' Association, which consisted entirely of certificated teachers. With reference to leave of•absence, if the staff of the school covered the needs of the school without a teacher he was -allowed to b 3 absent to attend the meeting. Alluding to the question of a teacher sitting on a committee which fixed his salary. Councillor Upson said it was im- possible for one member to get all the other members to give him a larger salary than the others. .

Councillor BENNETT said he differed from the com- mittee on the matter and should support the amend- ment. There was,a great deal of force in the difficul- ties which Alderman Monks had raised. He (the speaker) did not, share the extreme views taken by Conncillors Lord And Pemberton, but all the same he was rather surprised that they should have supported the amendment. He thought the Council would be ex- tremely foolish not to avail themselves of the best ex- pert advice that was possible, and obviously teachers and masters would be of the greatest conceivable service.

Councillor CRAIK said the members of the Teachers' I Union were mostly headtnasters and they would choose a headmaster froultheir own body to represent therh. Tile statement of Alderman -illo.nks ! was entirely misleading, as he was leading the Council • to believe that the meMbers -of the Union were going to send any kind of a teacher on the committee. Up to the present time 38 Education Committees had been formed, and no fewer than :33 had co-opted members of the National Union of Teachers. Alderman SUTTON could not see his way to support ; the amendment, because he did not think it was at all desirable that anyone holding an office of profit in a school should sit on the committee. It WAS just pos- sible tie might have some influence with the members of the Committee in matters in which he WAS personally Iconcerned,and he thought it was just as desirable in that case as any other case that the parity of the Council ; should be maintained. (Laughter). I Councillor PEACOCK, whilst approving the action of I the committee in leaving the matter for the Council to I decide, supported the amendment. Councillor W. ROBERTS pointed out that the Liverpool and Manchester City Councils had co-opted members of the Trades and Labour Councils. Surely, with such examples as those the Council might fairly appoint one representative from the Trades Council.. Alderman BOLTON thought it would avoid friction if they allowed each body as well £IS the Friendly Societies to have a representative. The working classes would, however, have direct representation through the mem- bers of the Town Council who would be elected on the Education Committee, as immediately anyone ceased to be a member of the Council he also ceased to be a mem- ber of the committee.

_ Councillor JOHN ROBE trrs said he•hqd not the slightest objection to the Trades Council having a representative, but he strongly objected to the Workingmen's Com- mittee being struck off.

Councillo-r F. R. ROBERTS said he understood the. Ironworkers were not represented on the Trades Coun- cil. They formed such a large proportion of the work- ingmen of Warrington that if they did not recognise the Trades Council, it was not representative of the work- ingmen. He was not in favour of striking off the Workingmen's Committee and substituting a commit- tee which was not recognized by the most powerful industry in the town. _

COMl;itiOr PIERPOINT pointed out tkat-the Trades Council had a direct representation of between 2,000 and 3,000 trade unionists and that was a body of men which the Town Council could not afford to ignore. He proposed that the Trades Council and the Workingnieu's Committee be represented. Councillor CRAIK seconded. CottncillorßENNETT was not in favour of the proposi- tion, as he considered they would be making the Com- mittee altogether too large. He was of the opinion that the Trades Council should be represented in pre- ference to the Workingmen's Committee. If they co-opted a representative from the Chamber of Com- merce which represented the bulk of the manufacturers, it followed as a natural:corrolary that they should co-opt a member of a body representing the workingmen. He contended that the Trades Council was•more represen- tative than the Workingmen's Committee. ,ACouncillor BURTON said the Workingmen's:Committee represented over 100 works. With regard to the Trades. Council he was informed that there was not a mu at the works with which he was connected or at Dallam or Bewsey who was in any way connected with the Council. He was in favour of the Friendly Societies being represented. He did not want to speak dis- paragingly of any society of workingmen but he saw the leading men at the works with which he was con- nected, and they told him that two of them were ap- pointed by the men asrepresentatives on the TradesCounci I bitt that they left because the Council was composed not altogether of workingmen but of insurance agents. They found it almost unbearable to attend the meetings_ and consequently withdrew from the. Council.

Alderman SUTTON was proceeding. to speak against the appointment of so many representatives on the com- mittee when he was interrupted by Councillor Tinnion. Turning to Councillor Tinnion the Alderman asked "Do you know what I am going to say ?" Councillor TINNtoN : Yes. (Laughter.) Alderman SurroN: What am I going to say? Per- haps it is something wiser than you have got in your head. (Laughter.) At any rate ,you will perhaps all ow me to say what I have to say. Continuing, thl Alder- man said the most important point was the representa- tion of the various schools, and the workingmen were represented in the appointments which have already been made. If so many appointments were made it would only cause difficulties. There would be so many contentions arising at the committee meetings and so many proposi- tions by the members that it would be almost impos- sible to get through the work. What they wanted to do was to co-opt the most efficient men who would repre- sent the. whole borough.. . _ .

Councillor JAMES EVANS said he was informed that the leaders of the ironworkers had expressed a desire that as far as they were concerned the representation should be through the Trades Council. He was also given to understand that a considerable number of mem- bers of the Amalgamated Society of Engineers,Aitters &c., were employed in the ironworks and they were re- presented on the Trades Council. He contented that the Trades Council had it prior claim to representation. Councillor PEMBERTON considered that •as Town Councils throughout the country had recognized Trades and labour Con noils,it wasfoaly right that the Warrington Trades Council should have representation. Councillor TINNION was in favour of each organization being represented. Alderman MONKS objected to any further enlargement of the committee. Already it would number twenty Six and if they had a School Board they would only have to have about twelve members. If they agreed to further enlargement the Council would be creating a very un- weildy committee. It did not follow that they were going to get good committee men. The representative appointed by an organization might not be of any use as an. educational reformer or have had any experience in educational matters in War- rington at all. He was now of the opinion that of the two organizations mentioned the Working- men's Committeewonld really give them a more repre- sentative committee of 'workingmen. Nearly all the works in town subscribed to the Infirmary and those who subscribed nominated two members of the com- mittee each year. In that way they got a representative of all the works, which they Would not get if they selected the Trades Council as the body to nominate a representative. The Workingmen's Committee had long been established and in a sense was on a firmer'hasis and foundation than the Trades Council. If in five years the . Trades Council still continued, and by that time included all the labour representatives the •scheme could be altered by permission of the Board of Education and the Trades Council substituted for the Workingmen's Committee. On a vote being taken it was decided that each organiz- ation should have a representative.

WORN OUT HEARTS. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills Cure.

WHEN' we remember (says a medical author) tivt from, the moment of birth the Heart pulsates about seventy times every minute it is not surprising to learn that Hearts_ wear out Oftener than is suspected. Seventy; beats a minute-4,200 an hour—over 100,000 a thy—con- siderably over thirty-six and a 'half millions a year. i No wonder there is unsuspected Heart Disease n the world ! Does your arm sometimes grow numb? Do your eye- lids tremble? Do you, now and then, experience a.. : momentary faintness and loss of comprehension? That probably means that the heart is a little worn. Are you • easily out of breath? When you lie down to sleep at 'night does your breath for a little while comeshort and ! fast, and do you notice a throbbing at the temples? ; The heart again. Are you flushed after eating, your heart beating rather too violently, your hands and lips swollen as by a temporary dropsy? That again is the Heart troubling you. The heart is very apt to be dis- turbed by outside causes, as Indigestion, Anwmia, the after-symptoms of Influenza, Fever (especially Scarlet Fever); Rheumatismor Gout. In any of these cases, or I where' the heart shows signs of sharing a general weak- ness of the system, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are a great protection. They are not purgative (you must on no•account take purgatives if your heart is even' under suspicion), but, on the contrary, strength- ening and tonic: Mr. Walter Stansfleld, 9, Bleak-street, Gomer.sal, near Tieed:s, says that Dr. Williams'Pink Pills brought him back fi-orn the very brink of the grave. He thus described his: cure in the Leeds SaturckLY Journal :

"I owe my lifetoDr. Williams' Pink Pills for Palo People. 'About eighteen months ago I began to feet pains about my heart—sometimes as if a rod of cold iron had been thrust into my chest. • "After a while I found it difficult to walk up a hill, however easy the incline might be. Doctors told me to be very careful; not to get excited, and never attempt to lift a heavy weight. The colour had gone from my face, my feet and legs became puffed. I tried to re— sume work again, but it was a failure. Nothing a- vailed to give me ,relief. But a niece, who called one day, declared that Dr. Williams' Pink pills had done: her great good. I began to use them taking one pill / after each meal. When the third box was nearly fin- ished T began to feel and to know for certain that a. miracle had been worked. The Heart trouble ceased, the swelling of my legs, feet, and hands disappeared, and to the amazement of our neighbours, I rapidly mended' until I became as you see me, a healthy man of forty-fi've, with a splendid appetite and fit for daily- toil. " •

Genuine pills bear the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, on the wrapper. If tradespeople worry with substitutes please send their names to Dr. Medibine Company, Holborn Viaduct,London, from whom genuille Dr. Williams' pills can .be had, post free, for 2s: 9d.—Six boxes, 13s. 9d.

A. Warrington Drowning Mystery. - -

Mr. S. Brighouse, County Cowroner, conducted arr. inquest at the Police Station on Monday afternoon, touching the death of James Eddie, a scavenger Nrr the employ of the Corporation, who resided in Win- wick-road, and whose body was recovered from the - -Mersey. near Quay Fold, about noon on Sunday.,--ThEy evidence showed that deceased left the house on Sun- day morning with the intention of .going for a walk, and at noon Sergeant Smith recovered his body from. the river. There were no marks of violence upon it. Deceased's son stated that his father had been subject to fits of dizziness during the past six months, but witness had never heard him threaten to take awar his life.—A verdict of " Found drowned " was re- rimed.

GOOD TEMPLAaT CONVENTION.

The annual session of the LO.G.T.. No. 1, Convention W. Lancashire was held in the Co-operative Hall, •Earle-street, Earlestown on Saturday. Bro. J. Parr, t.C.T., (Leigh) presided over a good attendance of re- plesentatives and visitors a large number journeying from Warrington and others being present from Ashton, Wigan,Widnes, Sutton, and Leigh. A vote of sympathy , Was accorded to Bro Speakman, C. Treas., (Ashton) on kis meeting with an accident. Bro. Parr gave a very good report of the past mouth's work in the area and s,tated that there seemed to be a grand revival of Work 111 the lodges, each one striving against the stream of intemperance.—The report of the C. Secretary (Bro. P. Smith) showed a substantial increase in most of the lodges in the area, the highest increase being 14 • in the Temperance Mission Lodge, Warrington. Sister Woods. C.S.J.T. (Earlestown), reported on the 'work among the juveniles which showed a fair state of affairs in this department. An increase of 20 was re- Ported of the Young Mission Temple, Warrington. The convention had been well represented in the National temple examination, a good number of juveniles gaining certificates. Bro. Vint, C.E.S. (Ashton) reported on the electoral work of the •convention. The officers were -elected 'ls follows :—C.C.T., 13.-o. Parr (Leigh); Bro. G. Clark Earlestown); C.E.S., Bro. Vint (Ashton); c.s.J.T., Sister Mrs. Woods (Earles- town) ; C.V.T., Sister Battersby (Leigh); C.Sec., Bro. Smith (Ashton) ; (.Treas., Bro. Waroarton (Earles- 'town) • C. Chaplain, Tiro. Hulme (Leigh) ; C. Marshall, tro. C. J. Sheath (Warrington); C.A7S., Sister Lowe Tu'igan); C.D.M. Sister N. Bowyer (Warrington); C. ard, Bro. Blundell (Wigan): C. Sentinel, Bro. Rogers, (Leigh). The ocers w.re• duly installed by Bro. the Rev. J. A. Lee, D.C.T. (Liverpool) who afterwards de- livered a brief address. A letter of sympathy was .ordered to be sent to Bro. Morris Jones, D. Sec., (Liverpool) on his serious illness. The.inembers then .'sat down to a substantial tea.. A public meeting was -afterwards held in the same room, the Rev. George Cook presiding. Earnest addresses were delivered by the Chairman and the Rev. J. A. Lee. Gramaphone selections by Mr. Porter, songs by Mr. Rogers, and Mrs. Williams and a recitation by Mr. Rose were much en- joyed. Two very pleasing feature.; were given by the Members of the "Young mission of Warrington" 4lnvenile Templ,, viz., " Mower dance" by the ~irls,and "Marching song" and drill by tae boys. The usual votes of thanks followed and the singing of the Doxology terminated the .proceedings.

,SOZONNT

BAND OF HOPE WORK.

Annual Mnting of the Lancashire and

Cheshire Union.

Canon Hicks, who is a leader of the Band of Hope movement in Lancashire and Cheshire, speaking- on Saturday, maintained that the amazing awakening amongst the magistrates was due to the long continued efforts of all temperance workersover a large series of years. . Referring to the progress made by recent legislation towards the temperance movement, he said that the hope- ful sign of the times was that the speed of progress would be much accelerated in the near future. Although we were said to be a nation of shopkeepers, John Bull could not be very careful in keeping his accounts,other- wis e he would have found that the most expensive thing to keep, were soldiers abroad and brewers at home. The occasion was the annual meeting in Manchester of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope and Tem- perance Union, which was established 40 years ago, and now links together 78 town and district unions,compris- ing 1,657 societies and 268,000 members. It was in- creased during last year by six unions and 103 bands of hope. An important p trt of the union's work is the Scientific Temperance Teaching Scheme, under which last year lecturers visited 1,163 schools and gave science object lessons to 3,253 teachers and 111,688 scholars, of whom 49,095 prepared essay reports of the lessons, for which 23,664 certificates of merit were awarded by the com- mittee. The village agents of the union tarried on a systematic missionary crusade in the northern, central, and southern districts, and visited 212 villages, and ad- dressed 254 meetings, with an aggregate audience of 33,881. • A resolution was on Saturday adopted agreeing to make every effort to raise £15,000 for the continuance for the next five years of teaching in the day and even- ing schools of Lancashire, Cheshire, ond North-West Derbyshire the principles of temperance and also for the provision, free from debt, of the new offices of the union in.Deansgate, Manchester.

HELENS.

It was Warrington's luck- to be drawn away on Satur- day, but they could not complain about having to visit St. Helens,the wooden-spoonists of the League. ‘s the team was at full strength with the exception of Fish, the 1,500 spectators who accompanied Warrington thought they would just about win. St. Helens were not despondent, however, and with Pennington in place of Hall at half-back, the team could not be said to be weakened.- A strong wind blew from goal to goal, hut otherwise the conditions were satisfactory. There would be 3,000 spectators present, and, the players and officials were:

St. Helens—Foulkes, back ; Liversage, C‘reevey, Kennedy, and Wearing, three-quarter backs; Penning- ton and Banks, half backs ; Briers Carney, Whiteley, Thompson, Green, Masyson, Mavily, and Smith, for- wards.

Warrington—Hallam, back; Harris,lsherwood,Dicken- son, and Hockenhall, three-quarter backs; Duckworth and Burgess, half backs ; Morrison, Boardman, Swift, Harmer Roberts, Edmondson, Naylor and Foster, forwards.

Referee, Mr. J. H. Smith, Widnes. Touch judges, Mr. W. J. Britton, Leigh, and Mr. T. L. Brimelow, Salford.

1 St. Helens won the toss and secured the assistance of the wind for the first 40 minutes, Swift starting for " Warrington at 3.15, a quarter of an hour having been I spent in waiting for Burgess. The first "scrum" was ' at centre, and Burgess punted, but the ball was blown down to Hallam Who saved in front of the St. Helens forwards. St. Helens at once attacked, but Duckworth ! and Dickenson relieved, the latter evequallymaking a 1 fine rim into St. ;Helens territory. Here the visitors !again got possession and Duckworth after a short run threw wide to Forster who fielded cleanly and making a 1 great effort scored at the corner amid deafening cheers from the Warrington partisans. Hallam failed at goal. This early score - against the wind seemed to give Warrington long odds, , but ; the Saints were not done with and very quickly I forced Warrington to the line. Here some very hard scrummaging took place, and eventually Banks dropped a goal vt,fy smartly. Still maintaining the advantage, chiefly by means of the wind, St. Helens kept Warring- ton defending. Creevy had hard lines, being only I knocked into touch a. yard from the line by Harris-. ! This scrimmage was followed by a most exciting period, the ball travelling •backwards and forwards' across the Warrington line which was only kept clear by resollite tackling. At last Waring made a mark from a fair Catch, and Pennington gave his side the lead by placing a good goal. St. Helens, thus encouraged, played up. The game was confined to the Warrington quarter and Warrington were given several free kicks, but these were of no practical utility against the wind. The game was twice stopped by injuries. St. Helens • now played five, three-quarters, Briars, being brought out of the Scrimmage, and still the seven forwards held the Warrington men. St. Helens lost a chance by an informality at the scrimmage. Hockenhall saved a certain try from Waring. Kennedy and Briars- ran strongly, but the passing was not taken up, and a grand opportunity was lost. Warrington then got away in the loose, Forster, Edmondson amid Harmer :casing the pressure on the Warrington goal. Banks - kicked over the scrimmage, and ran, but just failed to catch the ball, or he would have scored. Warrington were now penalized for the first time in the match, but Pennington missed the goal. Warrington were penned, The St. Helens left wing had a grand chance, but the passing was too slow. Warrington were again penalized, and Foulkes this time dropped a grand goal, placing his side three points ahead. Restarting, Duckworth ran well, but the ball went quickly to the Warrington goal, where Banks gave Briars possession, and he was only brought up a few inches outside the goal line. Half- time: St. Helens 3 goals, Warrington I try. On re-starting the Saints still kept Briers out as an extra threequarter. Warrington were some time before they could make headway, so energetic were the home scrummagers. At last a position was obtained near the home line, but the ball would not come out on the War-

RUNCORN v. WIDNES.

The first round in the cup ties was played between the above clubs on the Irwell Lane enclosure. A vast' amount of interest was taken in the match fully two thousand making the journey from the Lancashire side 1 of the river via the bridge. A quarter of an hour before the time the stand and, practically all the accommoda- tion round the field was occupied, there being nearly five thousand present. The climatic conditions were by no means as favourable as one might have wished, the ground being heavy,and the weather dull with a stiff breeze blowing from end to end. Both clubs turned out rep- resanthtive taams Mr. Kennedy of Broughton Rangers having charge of the whistle. Runcorn lost thetoss,Farmer starting,and Widnes at once forced Houghton to kick dead. Douglas marked, Moran a little later making a grand run along touch. Price got away, but Whelan marked and Aspey foend touch well in the home lines. The leather was kept at half way. At this point the game was held up for some time owing to Douglas having kicked Jolley-a caution following as a necessary consequence. By easy stages, mainly by forward play, the leather was carried into the 'visitors territory, Price and Butterworth being conspicuous with smart play. Houghton was next called upon, Whelan being tackled before he could part. A penalty kick taken by Tilley missed the cross bar by inches. Scrimmaging at half way gave Widnes a chance, smart play by Lally nearly getting Widnes in. Widnes still kept up the pressure, the home team contentinr, themselves with purely defen- sive tactics. At last after an exciting bit of play. Richardson gathered from Jolley, handing in turn to Butterworth and Jones, who transferred the play to the other end. For feet up, Runcorn were penalized, the ball travelling back again with a fine kick from Aspey. A brilliant little run by Butterworth placed Runcorn in atter position, but Tilley relieved. At this point Peacock was warned for foully charging Houghton. Richardson again set his line in motion, and Moran made a fine effoit to get in at the corner, Aspey just spoiling the effort. A forward rush by the Chemicals was saved on the line, Rispan being held by Butter- worth. The visitorsstill kept up the pressure, and from a good position Douglas landed a fine goal. The game warmed up considerably after this, Moran getting away grandly along touch. Tilley just as finely stopping his career. Tilley found touch, and gave the horuesteraan anxious time, Houghton eventually clearing. Play followed to half time, Widnes leading by one goal to Runcorn nil.

The restart saw a brilliant bit of passing by thehome back, touch being found at the top corner. From the scrummage Prise neatly gathered the leather and•dropped a. magnificent goal. Runcorn still kept up the attack Tilley mulling. The ball was kept unusually close, touch at length being found at the corner flag. Farmer failed at a penalty. Runcorn again got to work but the defence was very stubborn and no im- pression could be made. After a lot of unexciting. . play a round of passing between the home backs resulted in a scrummage being formed on the line. Jolley got the ball and handed to Richard- son,Butterworth getting over the line after a fineeffort. Farmer added the necessary goal points. The Chemicals got to work with a will after this, Butter- worth mulling and giving them a chance. Jolley worked the ball away, exchanges placing Runcorn in a much better position. For feet up the Chemicals were again penalized, Farmer failing at goal. Both sides continued to keep the ball close, little interesting play being wit- nessed. Final : —Runcorn, two goals one try; Widnes a goal.

Re-played Tie. SWINTON V. LEIGII.

These clubs, having played a draVn game at Leigh last Saturday, met again on the Swinton ground on Wednesday afternoon. The play throughout was in favour of the visitors, who won, but by the narrow margin of two points. This success was entirely due to the superiority of the Leigh forwards, who controlled the scrummages and allowed their backs to keep continually on the move. Bennett scored an unimproved try for Leigh, but before the interval Preston reduced the lead to one point by placing a penalty goal for Swinton. Afterwards Leigh continued to hold the upper hand,and Johnson farther augmented their score by placing two penalty goals. Swinton then played up better, and Lewis, from a round of passing, scored a try, which Preston failed -to improve. Up to the finish Leigh attacked strongly, but no further scoring took place, and the game ended—Leigh; two goals and one try (sev'en points); Swinton one goal and one try (five points).

FRIENDLY MATCH.

LATCHFORD RANGERS V. WIGAN "A."--7-These teams met at Wilderspool on Saturday before a poor attend- ance .of spectators. The visitors kicked off, but the Rangers were the first to attack, and Wigan being pena- lized Bate landedst goal in the first minute. Following this Latchford again got going, but the danger was quickly removed, Wilcox taking play to centre. A neat kick by Brown put the Rangers again on the attack, and Fillingham, after making a good run, was .bowled into touch near the line. Aided by a free kick, the visitors gained a footing in the home half, and for the first time theltangers were forced to defend. Still keeping up the attack,the ball was dribbled over,but the referee ordered a scrimmage and for this Rigby scrambled over near the posts. . The kick at goal was abortive. The home team were ?gain seen to advantage, and Brown landed a goal. Half-time arrived with Latchford leading by four points to three. After the interval. Wigan were the first to press, but the home defence proved safe. Play ruled in mid-field for a time, until Green gathered the ball and after eluding several opponents scored in grand style, no goal resulting. The Rangers were now a beaten team, Wigan having all the play. The game was stopped for a few moments owing to an in- jury to one of the visitors. Close on time Wigan again got into their stride, and Wilcox cleverly out-manceuvr- ing Brown, scored under the posts. The kick at goal ended in the ball striking the upright and rebounding into play. The final score read as follows :—Wigan, nine points ; Latchford Rangers, four poials.

The Bold Burglary.

At Liverpool Assizes on Tuesday, William Glover (49), painter, pleaded guilty to burglary at Bold, on the sth inst., and stealing clothing and other pro- perty belonging to James Warburton. Prisoner was discovered in the house by Mr. Warburton, and after a struggle was detained for the police. There were several previous convictions against him, one of six years' penal servitude for housebreaking. He was now sentenced to three years' penal servitude.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must inall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication in, be written i on one side only of the paper and sent n, f possible,not later than Thursday. _ _

Correspondents lire strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the manuscripts which are not used.
