Warrington Investigation Society.

Sir,—l have been requested to publicly express my views with regard to the proposals for altering the con- stitution of this society. I am, and have always been strongly opposed to any change, but as the names of some of my principal supporters were mentioned among the patrons of a proposed new society, I did not wishto press my petsOnal views. I now find that all my best supporters prefer matters to continne as at present, and I have definitely decided to be guided by their Wishes.

I understand that it was stated at one of the meetings that I warmly welcomed a proposed paid Investigator. I have never altered my opinion that a paid officer is entirely unnecessary, but in deference to the strongly expressed views of others, I agreed to waive my objec- tion, and after consulting with my principal supporters, Ihave decided that I will find some one to 'in restigate any outside case at a charge of 2/6 per case. I propose to continue the work of the Society with- out a committee, as experience has proved that a com- mittee either becomes merely formal, or owing to the routine and delay which it necessarily causes, it hampers the usefulness of the Society. . _

The Society will be nnder control of the president and officers, the accounts will be duly audited, and the assistance of voluntary workers will always be welcomed. _

I should like to take this opportunity of tendering my warmest thanks to the subscribers, for the great support they have been to the Society, and for their great kindness to me,—Yours faithfully. B. A. HARRISON. 6, Woodbine Terrace, Latchford, 19th, February 1903.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Rugby Football Club.

Sir,—After their spasmodic performance of Satur- day last against St. Helens I venture to think it's high time for something to be said and done to again raise the prestige of the club to its once high standard of excellence. It has been a general cry all round of the weakness of our forwards this season, but unfortunately nothing has been done to remedy this defect. Matches innumerable I may say have been lost simply because the front rank have been beaten from possession, and the backs have been compelled to act on the defence for fully three parts of the genie. lam sure it is more than galling to the man behind, for we all know that if the forwards could get possession our backs are as capable as any playing in the Northern Union. I dare say when my remarks are scanned over by the officials of the club they will say to one another "Oh let him come and try his hand to procure players." My own personal opinion on the matter is this. Players can be got if the club officials will pay for them. I can point to several clubs (Wigan for instance) who are constantly signing on good play- ers and surely why should this not be done at Warring- ton. The club I am given to understand is hi a firm financial position and surely would it not be a far better investment to get a few good men thanput their annual profit in some corporation stock. I have just been informed that the club have sent a deputation down south in the hope of securing some players. What on earth good will this do? We have played the princi- pal match of the season and as we all well know we lost. Oh what splendid strategy of the officials! I begin to wonder why their services were not solicited for commanding positions as leaders in the late Trans- vaal war. I think I have now said enough on the management of the club and I hope my remarks will for the beirefit of the supporters help to awaken the officials out of their perilous position which they now occupy. If lam not trespassing too much on your valuable paper, I should like to touch upon a point with regard to the works com Petitions. If there are to be any more of these competitions I might suggest that the committee when drawing up a set of rules specify very clearly points that cannot be turned by the various teams to their mutual benefit. The best suggestion I should say would be for each club to send in a list of players by a certain date and the committee to run over each one most carefully and strike out all players that are not qualified. This method would save any more unpleasant protests such as we had last year. These few lines I have penned out of personal disgust and I hope that time will bring the long wished for improvement. navi DISGUSTED SUPPORTER. Mr. A. H. Crosfield and Mr. Joynson- -0,44 Hicks. Sir,—You printed last week a letter in which I made some attempt to defend the view which I have taken of the origin of the South African war and the merits of Mr. Chamberlain's policy from the criticisms which Mr. Joynson-Hicks was pleased to make upon me in a recent speech. I propose in this letter to examine with some care the rival theory of history which Mr.Joynson- Hicks has himself put forward. Mr. Joynson-Hicks' historical method is to fix his attention on a single in- cident which occurred twenty-two years ago in the tangled relations between Briton and Boer, while he resolutely closes his eyes to all that went before the affair at Majuba or followed after. The story of our dealings with the Boers began in fact a century ago. Our first contact was not exactly happy. We so mismanaged the [emancipation of the slaves of the Colony that we drove the pioneers who founded the late Republics to quit our dominions very much in the spirit which animated the children of Israel when they escaped from Pharoah. They settled in Natal, than a desolate wilderness, only to be driven out at the bayonet's point. A Conservative ministry annexed their settlement in, the Free State, only to restore its independence later. Another Conservative Government annexed the Trans- vaal in 1877, apparently under the impression that the Boers would welcome British rule,and that first mistake it aggravated by declining year after year to fulfil its promise of immediate self-government. All this hap- pened before Majuba, as the Raid happened afterwards. The aged President Kruger had played his part as a boy in the great exodus from the Cape, witnessed as a lad the annexation of Natal, opposed in his prime the first annexation of the Transvaal, and quashed in his old age Dr. Jameson's abortive attempt to seize the Re- public. Had there been no Majuba, here were memories enough to foster suspicions and kindle hates. History may begin for Mr. Joynson-Hicks in 1881. The Boers unluckily had longer memories, and their annals warned them against a party in this country which had never ceased to covet their country and threaten their independ- ence. When Mr. Chamberlain met their concessions with menaces in the summer of 1899, and Lord Milner retorted on their most "promising" reforms (the word is the Colonial Secretary's) with a demand for "extreme measures," they had some excuse for supposing that these statesmen were about to repeat the 1877 policy of grab and the premature raid of Mr. Rhodes.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME NEGLECTED FACTS.

But let us come to closer terms with Mr. Joynson- Hicks' charge of "cowardice" against Mr. Gladstone for his action in restoring the independence of the Transvaal after the affair of Majuba—a policy of which, as Mr. Parker has reminded us, the Duke of Devonshire (then Lord Hartington) and Mr. Chamberlain were the mostleloquent advocates. Mr. Joynson-Hicks' researches into the history of this period have not been exactly extensive and it may be well to remind him of two facts. In the first place the real responsibility for the errors of this period lay with Sir hartle Frere who reported, as our agent on the spot, that the Boers would welcome annexation. The Boers in fact protested, at first peaceably by petitions and deputations. Sir Bartle Frere retorted that the protests came only from a small minority, that there was no real feeling or sincerity behind them, and that the mass of the people acquiesced in our rule. Mr. Gladstone accepted the statements as Mr. Disraeli had accepted them. He was undeceived when the Boers rose in arms, and with absolute unani- mity proved how genuine and passionate was their attachment to liberty. Then too late to avoid bloodshed, Mr. Gladstone realised that his whole policy had been founded on a grave misunderstanding. He realised too that to enforce the annexation of the Transvaal meant something more than the perpetuation of a mistake by coercion. It meant the alienation of the Cape Dutch and a quarrel with the Free State. There was a risk of a rising in the Cape Colony, while the Free Staters were growing ominously restive. Mr. Gladstone then resolved to make terms with the Trans- vaal Boers in order to save South Africa from thepiti- able consequences of a racial war. That decision was taken before Majuba. Was Mr. Gladstone to reverse his policy because a few British soldiers had been worsted in a skirmish so trivial that it would have passed almost unnoticed had it occurred during the late war? Mr. Joynson-Hicks answers "yes," and stigma- tises the hesitation of a statesman who shrank from light- ing the flames of a civil war as an act of " cowardice."

===== 0002601_19030220_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
A MODERN PARALLEL.

It would be interesting to test the sincerity of this view by enquiring what view Mr. Joynson-Hicks took of a rather similar transaction in which Lord Salisbury was involved. It happened no further back than 1895. We were then engaged in a rather sordid dispute with Venezuela for the possession of certain gold-fields. We were in the right asa subsequentiobitration proved,and had nothing whatever to fear from the investigation of our claims. Venezuela pressed us to arbitrate,but Lord Salisbury stubbornly refused, and declared his intention to seize the disputed territory and to vindicate our right to it by war. Suddenly the United States inter- vened. Mr. Cleveland addressed a most menacing message to Congress. He threatened Lord Salisbury in the most unambiguous language, and gave him to under- stand that a war with Venezuelawould also be a war with the United States. Lord Salisbury, the Duke of Devonshire and Mr. Chamberlain acted precisely as Mr. Gladstone, Lord Bartington and Mr. Chamberlain had done in 1881. They de-

===== 0002601_19030220_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Friday, February 20, 1903.

cided that a war with the United States, which could by any means be averted, would be a crime. They ac- cepted the suggestion of arbitration which they had previously refused to entertain. It was not what Disraeli would.have called "a spirited foreign policy." Lord Salisbury was willing enough to fight little Vene- zuela; he shrank from fighting the United States. I wonder whether Mr. Joynson-Hicks calls him a "coward" on that account. And yet a man who blames Mr. Gladstone's conduct in 1881 ought also to censure Lord Salisbury's conduct in 1895. The scruples which Mr. Joynson-Hicks presumably approved when Lord Salisbury gave way to them were the same scruples which influenced Mr. Gladstone in 1881. There is no reason to suppose that either statesman was influenced by any doubt about the result of an appeal to arms. Our navy greatly outnambers the fleet of the United States, and in .1881 our military superiority over the 'Boers was more obvious than in 1899. In both cases, however, a victory would have cost us dear,b3r embitter- ing relations which ought to be cordial.

the country would be safe in the hands of a Liberal Minister. Ido not notice however, that they them- selves feel any great confidence in Mr. Brodrick's management of the Army. The "Times "has attacked his whole scheme of army reform persistently and stoutly. Mr. Winston Churchill has ridiculed the whole pretentious fabric, and I notice that even Mr. Marshall Hall, whose strong point is not originality or independ- ence, gently applauds the Member for Oldham's cam- paign. The truth is that Mr. Brodrick's imitation of German models ignores our real needs and is at once costly and inefficient. We are not a Continental Power and do not need a Continental Army. An Army which consists, as our experience in 1900 proved —to a very large extent of underfed and immature lads whom we dare not send abroad even when we most need them, clearly does not meet our needs. On the other hand, Mr. Brodrick had neglected the Volunteers and depleted the Reserves, and he demands so many millions for the Army that the needs of the Navy run great risk of neglect. To care for the Navy first is the guiding principal of Liberal policy in questions of defence ; to develop the volunteers it regards as our duty since we wish to avoid conscription and to obtain competent and educated officers for the Army it regards as more important than to add to the swollen numbers. of raw recruits.

Turning to general foreign policy, is Mr. Joynson- Hicks quite sure that the country really has such con- fidence in Lord Lansdowne? His chief exploits so far have been to congratulate Abdul-Hamid on his Jubilee and to conclude our precious Venezuelan alliance with Germany. I commend the "Spectator's" criticism in Saturday's issue on that fine sample of muddle to Mr. Joynson-Hicks' attention, but in truth he must be strangely deaf to political opinion if he has not heard from his own Tory friends the dissatisfaction and contempt they are expressing on all hands for this played-out Government. Does he not realise that the nation would be thankful at this moment to exchange Lord Lansdowne for Lord Rosebery, by far the ablest Foreign Minister we have now, as everyone knows. A Li befal Government would enjoy more influence abroad, simply because it would make fewer enemies. It would not include a member who in turn insults each one of our neighbours, compares Russia to the devil, threatens to teach France manners and bandies abuse with the German Chancellor. The problem of foreign policy, as Mr. Balfour put it at Liverpool the other day, is really the problem of attaining a nation's legiti- mate ends through the agency of the European Concert. It will not be claimed for Mr. Chan.ber- lain that he has added the number of our friends in that concert. His boast indeed is that he has made enemies of all the world. A Liberal Government would enjoy this advantage over a Jingo Ministry, that it would not aim at exciting animosities and kindling prejudices, nor would it be hampered by the suspicion and dislike of every democracy in Europe. To foster the navy and the volunteers, to respect the self-governing traditions of our great Colonies, to pur- sue abroad a policy, firer, 'indeed, but tolerant and unaggressive—these are Liberal traditions, and they are also the traditions which have made the Empire strong, loyal and free. THE RIGHT OF FREE SPEECH.

With Mi.-.--:374-11-s-on- =flicks I need not deal further. His evident determination to support the foreign policy and foreign wars of any Ministry in power, whether its work is good, bad or indifferent is enough to put him out of court, and it would be interesting to hear what North Manchester thinks of that political position. Mr. Joynson-Hicks is one of those who would have condemned Burke, Fox and Chatham as traitors because they denounced the American wax. The Joynson-Hicks's of that day lost us the American Colonies. Mr. Joynson-Hicks would doubtless have dealt out the same measure to Bright and Cobden who opposed our disastrous and futile Crimean war. And yet Lord Salisburyhas admitted that in that war we "put our money on the wrong horse." For my part I believe that the day will come when the nation will honour the courage and the foresight of Mr. Morley as it honours the memory of Burke and of Bright. But—and this is the second time I venture to ask, yes and to press, the question--are we sever to be wise ti// a century or half a century after the event? The doctrine that no patriot will ever in his day oppose any war,lowever disastrous, howeverunnecessary,place a iremendous and most dangerous power in the hands.; of any interest which may choose to exploit the docility of such indiscriminating patriotism (?) for its private ends. It is not the least ominous sign of the times that the opinions of such pre-eminent men as John Morley, Professor Bryce and Herbert Spencer have been con- demned not merely as erroneous but as something dis- graceful and unpatriotic. Sir Henry Campbell-Banner- man's courageous protest against farm burning was receivedat the time as something almost treasonable. Who rejoices to-day, when the heat of the fray is over, that we made those two ,Republics scarcely better than a wilderness? Sir H. Campbell-tannerman used language no stronger than the late Field-Marshal Sir Neville Chamberlain employed, and that great soldier's bra ve and powerful letters lam sure are not forgotten. No One quite dared to accuse him,the gallant veteran, covered with medals and scars, the very em- bodiment of the old gallant English traditions, of any lack of patriotism. They suppressed his protests silently. If Mr. Joynson-Hicks cares to accuse Herbert Spencer of folly and Sir Neville Chamberlain of treason, lam content to stand humbly in the same pillory. I cannot trespass further ,on your space this week, but with your kind indulgence may take another oppor- iunity to deal with some aspects of the South African situation of special and peculiar interest to the working classes.—Yours London, February 17th, 1903.

A. H. CROSFIELD.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RESULTS OF THE WAR,

The results of the war .from which Mr. Gladstonc, I shrank in 1881 are now before us, and it is only by clos- ing our eyes that we can congratulate ourselves upon the result. Mr. Chamberlain has himself compared the political condition which the war has brought about at the Cape to "comic opera." The Colony is in a condi- tion which differs little from civil war. The two races are divided by "suspicions" which even his eloquent speeches—and an energy and zeal we all admire—will not remove. On the one hand the English colonists are still clamouring for the suspension of the constitution, a remedy which could only occur to man who had given way to absolute despair. The Dutch, on the other hand, cherish so deep a resentment against:their English neigh- bours for their administration of martial law, that they are in many places organising an elaborate system of boycotting. Between the two races social intercourse is entirely suspended, and Dutchmen even decline to purchase goods in English shops. The Free State, which was before the war a prosperous community, orderly, well-governed and attached to its ample liberties is now in a deplorable condition, torn by . civil dissensions and racial hates. Mr. Chamberlain left it with the threat of General de Wet ringing in his ears to raise a" rebellion" against us, not an armed rebellion to be sure, but a revolt of protest and agitation. How poor the prospect of appeasement in the Transvaal is my be gauged from the fact that Generals Botha and Delarey have just refused to accept seats in the Legis- lative Council, because they have no confidence what- ever in Lord Milner'sadministration. Indeed he seems to possess the confidence only of one section of the popu- lation—the alien capitalists, the Beits, the Echsteins and the Albus, who lisp their patriotism in broken English. The professional and the middle classes are in revolt against his autocratic government, and the Johannesburg " Tribune " a labour paper con- ducted by men who fought as volunteers in the war, has regretfully registered its opinion that the working classes were better off under Mr. Kruger, who ,at all events forbade Sunday labour, and was on the I point of passing a Miners' Eight Hours Bill when the war broke out. As for the natives, their wages have fallen almost ,50 per cent., and their fate will be forced labour, unless indeed the mine-owners succeed in importing Chinese coolies, who will rob blacks and whites alike of their employment. The war in short has set race against race, class against classs. The capital- ists who financed the Jameson Raid alone stand to profit from the anarchy. It is true the Union Jack floats above the hatred and the misery, the devastation and disloyalty. But the Empire is not the stronger for the inclusion of this chaos within its borders. It would have been better for us could we have had the Boers to-day as friendly neighbours instead of dis- affected subjects. M. Gladstone foresaw these consequences when he declined to enter on a war of races in 1881. Mr. Chamberlain imprudently belittled them in 1899. We have all of us followed his South African tour with our best wishes and every Liberal in Warrington will endorse what our President Mr. Parker, has said on this subject. Liberals showed no petty party-spirit when they bade him God-speed, and Mr. Morley and Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman vied with Lord Rosebery in a generous and sincere ex- pression of good-will. Mr. Chamberlain has done what in him lay, and faced a tangled situation with admirable courage. But it is not in the power of one man, how- ever brave and energetic, to undo the reckless work of three years of provocation and desolation.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR AS HANDICAP.

If this war had done nothing worse than create for us problems in South Africa more menacing and difficult than any which lay before usat its outbreak,we should have cause enough to deplore the diplomacy which forced us into it. But its results will influence our home politics for a generation. The last session of Parliament did two notable things. It imposed a duty on corn and thereby took a great stride backwards to- wards protection. It also passed an Education Act. .1 was much impressed with. the defence which Mr. Mar- shall Hall gave of this measure at.the recent Unionist Demonstration in Warrington. " The onlyal ternative," he said, "was to buy up the Voluntary Schools. He would like to have seen them bought up, but who was to find the millions? The Government was over- burdened with taxation." In plain words, then the war has cost us so much, that we cannot afford to adopt the best solution of the educational problem. No ques- tion is so vital to our progress, or so essential if we are to maintain our industrial supremacy. But the war has so tied our hands that we must needs adopt what even Mr. Marshall Hall regards as a second-best solu- tion. Weare like a merchant who went to law when he might have arbitrated, and then discovered that he had spent on lawyer's bills the money that should have sent his sons to college. It would have cost no more than 15 millions at an outside estimate to buy up the Volun- tary Schools. But then we have spent 250 on our war. At every turn we shall find ourselves confronted with the same difficulty. There is a chance to-day of settling for ever the Irish Land Question by buying out the landlords, whose exactions have made that unhappy country the poorest and most disloyal province in the Empire. But again the financial question stares us in the face. We can none too well afford the generous treatment of Ireland in this matter, which is neverthe- less an absolute duty. We have entered on a period of depressed trade. With the unemployed all around us, the demand for old-age pensions is once more stirring the working-classes. But where is the money to come from? This beneficent measure was possible enough in 1895, when Mr. Chamberlain won a general election upon it. Instead of taking steps to realise his social programme however, he has spent in lyddite fumes the millions which would have assured a comfortable old age to the veterans of industry. WHAT IS A LITTLE ENGLANDER?

_ Mr. Joynson-Hicks is pleased to dub me aLittle Englander, on what evidence Ido not know. He means by that charge, so far as I can discover, that I care rather more than he appears to do, for the prosperity of I England and the welfare of its inhabitants. If to hold that the wealth we have squandered in a mischievous adventure at the other end of the earth would have been better spent in strengthening the hcart of Empire be Little Englandism the accusation does not distress me. Three-fourths of the white population of the Empire still reside in these islandsond he who would make the Empire great will achieve more by social reform in little England than by raids and wars in dis- tant Continents. The greatest Empire in the end will not be the Empire which inehides in its borders the greatest possible number of torrid deserts and tropical swamps. It will be the Empire whose citizens enjoy the most widely diffused comfort and the most efficient education. Patriotism, if Mr. Joynson-Hicks will believe me, has more to do with the housing of the working-classes and the improvement of elementary schools, than with the avenging of some sorry skirmish two-and-twenty years ago. We may feel some astonish- ment and even some shame when we find that Germany and America, France and Holland, poor Norway and little Switzerland have defeated us in the race for I popular education. SANE IMPERIALISM.

I profess myself a sane Imperialist. I am proud of the wealth and the power of the Empire and I am proud of its freedom. It is because I value before all else the liberties which it has carried into so many parts of the globe, that I doubt the wisdom of subjugating a white race which for many a long year we may have to govern on autocratic lines and evemwe bya costly and threatening display of over- whelming force. Mr. Joynson-Hicks confounds this view with Little Englandism. He never made a greater mistake than in bringing such a charge against a War- ringtonian. Warrington is about the very last place to produce Little Englanders. No town in the world of its size sends so remarkable a range of manufactures to all parts of the globe. Ever since I went to business I have been engaged in the export business of one of our local firms who do this trade in foreign markets. Does Mr. Joynson-Hicks think it likely that after so many years of this schooling one could possibly undervalue the importance of national power and prestige in its re- lation to and its protection of our vast commerce—that soramerce which the life-blood of our whole oountry ; bnt even the trade of our own day and of the future, vitally important as it is, is not everything. There are other conditions which prompt our loyalty to what Lord Rosebery has well termed the larger patriotism ;* kinship and pride of race and honourable traditions. -Yes, we grant all this and grant this as a whole, but do not let us confuse prestige and its counterfeit, the Imperialism and the spurious coin, of which so much has been put in circulation the last eight years both in this country and in South Africa. Will anyone contend that the events which have revealed themselves there in the history of these later years have added either to our prestige or moral weight and authority in the world? The loyalty and patriotism of our Colonies have indeed been proved to us, but did any of us doubt them? On the other hand, our finances are very seriously disorganised .and I do not no- tice that our neighbours are inspired wfth a very deep respect for the foresight of our statesmen or the capa- city of our War Office. To belong to a powerful State is of the first importance by a trader; to belong to a State which happens to be governed by a passion for reckless methods, violent self-assertion and the widest extravagance is, on the contrary, a most serious handicap. Our "sort of war " in Venezuela, for example,has made us so unpopular in that unlucky republic that our mer- chants have to face the probability that a great deal of their business will now pass to American firms. It is the policy of Tory orators at this moment to persuade the country that neither the defence nor the prestige of

Knocked Down by a Tramcar. A woman named Ellen Kaife, aged 30 years, of no fixed abode, was knocked down bv a tramcar in Liver- pool-road on Sunday might. Her head was severely injured and she was conveyed to the Infirmary in an unconscions condition. BEST BOOTS FOR ALL.—UNDER the CLOCK.—Advt. Resignation of the Rev. E. W. Lummis.

A fortnight ago we annomiced that the Rev. E. W. Lummis, M.A., -had declined an invitation to take charge of an important Unitarian Chapel hi Leicester. The invitation came from the congregation of the Great Meeting House, and on the receipt of Mr, Lummis's decision, a deputation was appointed to wait upon him and ask him to reconsider his decision. After, careful re-consideration Mr. Lummis has de- cided to accept the offer and on Sunday last he placed his resignation in the hands of Mr. Davies, the secretary of the Cairo-street congregation. At a meeting of the committee held subsequently the letter wasiread and a resolution passed acknowledging the services of Mr. Lummis, and regretfully accepting his resignation. It was decided to release Mr. Lummis from his duties at Cairo-street by the end of March, and the rev, gentleman will, by that time, have com- pleted a two years' pastorate at Cairo-street. At Leicester he will succeed the Rev. H. Gow, 8.A., who has accepted a*pastorate in London.

Compensation for The Loss of an Eye. _ At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, be- fore his Holton. Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C. Mr. T. S. Steel made an application on behalf of William Kettle, 44, Laira-street, for an order against Messrs. Francis Morton and Company, Ltd., engineers and contractors, Garston, for the payment of 15s. per week during applicant's incapacity owing to injuries received.—Mr. Steel explained that on April 22nd last the applicant was in the employ of the respondent company as a boiler maker, when a steel chipping flew into his eye and injured it. He was incapacitated for several months and application was made for compensation under the Workmen's Compensation Act, the respondents Consenting to pay 15s. per week. In August last applicant was able to resume his employment and the agreement as to com- pensation was suspended without prejudice to any further claim which might be made. In November his eye became worse, and applicant had to leave work and undergo an operation, the eye being extracted. He now applied for the revival of the original agree- ment to the payment of 15s. per week-, and respondents had filled a notice agreeing to this. He asked his Honour to make the formal award.-:--His Honour granted the order with costs.

THE GREEN DRAGON LICENCE.

TM Co-operative Hall, Winwick-street, was the scene on Friday of a meeting of protest against the recent transfer of the licence of the Green Dragon Inn, Bridge- street to the " Brooklands," Lovely-lane. pir. C. HALEY presided at the outset over an attend-, mice of about 100 persons. He was accompanied on the platform by the Rev. J. F. Matthews, Rev. Herbert Dunnico, Councillor James Evans, Councillor T.Barton, Councillor W. Brough, Mr. T. Dale,Mr. S. B. Stringer and Mr. Griffiths.

• The CHAIRMAN explained that the absence of Mr. C. E. Parker who had been announced to preside, was due to the fact that he had to attend another meeting. LErrERs OF APOLOGY.

===== 0002601_19030220_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REV. JOHN YONGE AND ThE ACTION

OF THE BENCH. Several letters of apology for non-attendance were read :

The Rev. JOHN YONGEwrote:-Jf Dear Mr. Haley. As I told you on Wednesday, it is more than doubtful whether I can get to the meeting. I have not yet solved the problem of being in several places at one and the same time, and my duties to-night leave me no choice as to where I must of necessity be. I am afraid it is rather late in the day now to make any very effective "protest" about this Green Dragon business. But the decision of the magistrates affords a striking object lesson of the evil of ' a law which allows an obnoxious business to be forced upon a neigh- bourhood which has declared itself against its introduc- tion. What moral right can a bench of magistrates have to sanction such a procedure against the expressed wish and resolute opposition of the people most imme- diately concerned? None whatever! It is simply monstrous, and absolutely wicked. Further, what moral right have magistrates who are members of the Town Council—which body has promoted this business in order to make a good bar- gain with the brewers interested—to sit on the Bench and adjudicate on their own case? lam doubt- ful whether they have even legal right to do so, and should have thought that at least their doing so should have been strongly disputed. If they have such legal right, the law needs changing. They certainly have no moral right, and they should, from the mere sense of decency, have abstained from any such procedure. hope it will be made clear to them that a very largeand responsible section of their fellow townsmen absolutely condemn their action. We have tome in this town to a pretty pass! A wealthy brewing firm can get its way, if only it Will persist long enough. But a less wealthy firm may ask in vain ! Alas for Justices' justice ! My regret in this matter is very great indeed, and the opponents of this transfer of the Green Dragon have my entire sympathy. The- only thing I like is the name of the new hotel. Nothing better describes a public-house than the term "Green Dragon." But all the dragons should be cast out from civilized, not to say Christianized Society." Rev. R. WlLKixs REES, of BeW•sey House. wrote : "I greatly fear that it will be wholly impossible for me to attend your meeting to-night at the Co-oporative Hall. I have to preside at a very important Committee Medting of all the managers of the Wesleyan Day Schools in Warring- ton and neighbourhood, and I have no hope at present of being able to get away in time for your meeting. If I can come.' will. I sincerely hope you will have a well attended and most successful gathering and that the people will express in no uncertain fashion their condemnation of the extraordinary and wholly un- warrantable action of the Warrington magistrates in regard to the transfer of the Green Dragon licence to Lovely-lane."

iNfiS; BESSIE DUTTON wrote :—"I am extremely sorry to be absent from the protest meeting to-night, but I feel it an absolute necessity to raise nryvoice (on paper) and that of my association—the British Women's Tem- perance Association—against the granting of the licence of the Green Dragon. It was, I am sure, with the utmost surprise and real dismay that the majority of the Warrington people heard that they had magistrates who could and would disregard the wishes of their co- citizens inn given neighbourhood as to the planting of a licensed house in their midst even though those wishes were vehemently expressed, and had been almost pathetic in their persistency and unanimity. May your meeting have success in its object aid make its own legitimate impression." At a later stage of the proceedings Mr. Parker arrived and presided over the meeting. CRITICISED

Mr. HALEY commented upon the transfer and con- tended that the licences surrendered were of very little value. This feature of the question had aroused great indignation—that the Brewery Company even to-day were keeping alive a large number of licences for the express purpose of bargaining with the magistrates to secure better premises. Mr. Haley also strongly criti- cised the action of members of the sub-committee of the Corporation which dealt with the licence, who he stated were present on the Bench and helped to decide the application in favour of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Company. He contended that if the owner of the Brooklands bad presented the property to the ward for the purpose of a public recreation ground it would have been an action of common justice and gratitude for the great services rendered to him by the people of Whitecross Ward in persistently returning him to the Town Council unop- posed and placing him in the position he occupied to-day.. The speaker went on to allnde to the action of members of the Council who; he said, had posed as reformers. He referred to Alderman Shaw Green,who, he said, had over and over again expressed himself publicly in the town as being the friend of all that was good and righteous. Where had he been in connec- tion with this matter ? His face had never once been seen on the Bench when the application was considered He would refer to another person upon whom they had a great claim in Whitecross Ward—who had been sent to the Town Council for more than fifteen years as represehative of the Whitecross Ward and formerly of the North-west Ward. He referred to Mr. Henry Roberts. Over and over again he had been sent to the Council; they had championed his cause; and fought his battles and stood by him to a man. They had a right to expect from him sohie help and support in the matter. But where had he been? On every occasion he had been absent from the Court and they had not had the influence of his vote. When the whole thing was over and they had no power to appeal at Quarter Sessions, Mr. Henry Roberts anpeared at the Brewster Sessions. It was a great shame that people whom they had stood for and placed upon the pinnacle of fame and honour should turn their backs upon them and leave them in that hour of need. (Applause.) THE FIRST RESOLUTION.

The Rev.J. F. MATTHEWS 5 oved the following resolu ti on :—" That this meeting strongly condemns the action of the magistrates in granting the transfer of the Green. Dragon licence to Lovely-lane in direct opposition to the strongly expressed opinions and wishes of the in- habitants of the neighbourhood, and notwithstanding the repeated declaration in court of the Chief Constable that in his judgment no such licence was at all necessary. The meeting is of opinion that the magistrates, having no additional evidence to that tendered on two previous applications within the last three months,had no ground on whict: now to grant this transfer other than the pro- posal of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co., to forego two other licences in the borough, a proposal which public opinion deems hardly good enough to warrant the granting of this new licence in the teeth of such absolutely pronounced opposition on the part of those most immediately concerned."—The rev, gentleman characterised the action of Mr. Monks as " inconsistent and distinctly un-Christian," and the conduct of the magistrates as " Godless, reckless, and even fiendish." In ignoring the wishes of the people of the neighbour- hood ; in disregarding the statement of the Chief Con- stable that the licence was not required; and in altering their previous decisions on the matter, the magistrates deserved the execration and contempt of all fair and noble minded citizens. (Applause). The Rev. H. DtmNtco seconded. He contended that by granting the transfer and enabling Messrs. Greenall to over-ride the wishes of the people, the magistrates were over-riding every principle of justice and liberty which they were sent on the Bench to protect and enforce. (Applause.) Messrs. Greenall would rue the day when they agitated for the transfer. The fight had commenced, and he knew who was going to win'. He, for one, was going to fight the brewers to the bitter end and they would see whether the brewers could over-mle them in the way they had done. (Applause). Mr. C. E. PARKER, who • had now taken the chair, said he made no profession of being a teetotaler but he did make a profession of being loyal to the great principle of allowing people in any locality to say whether they wanted a public-house or not. The existence of a public-house caused discomfort, a reducing of the value of property and other .inconveniencies against which the com- munity had a right to. protest. When the people of a district had by a practically unanimous expression of opinion declared that they did not want a public-house, the magistrates-had no moral right to thrust that public- house upon the people. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously. THE SECOND RESOLUTION.

Connelllor ;TAMES EVANS then moved the following resolution :—"That this meeting further records its protest against the action of the • Corporation in this matter, and the appearance in court of the Deputy Town Clerk in support of the application." He pointed out that the Corporation as a whole had not been re- sponsible for the action taken by the officials and the ultimate success of the application. He was satisfied that if the Council had had an opportunity of voting on the matter they would have decided against the appear- ance of officials to support the application by a large majority. He maintained that when the Green Dragon licence had served its purpose it should have been allowed to die. (Applause)ll3l ' Councillor BARTON, in seconding the resolution, said he inquired of the Town Clerk upon whose authority' he made the statement before the Bench that the Cor- poration supported the applications, and he replied the old Property Purchasing Committee." It had caused grief to the residents in the Lovely-lane neigh- bourhood that they were to have this public-house thrust in their midst. Some of the residents had gone

Workingmen's Mission Sunday School. A very interesting and instructive lecture was given in connection with the above school last week by Miss Eva Wallington entitled" Homes made without Hands." The lecture was illustrated by limelight views, manipulated by Mr. Wallington. There WZIA a good attendance, and the lecturer, gave to her hearers, some very useful imformation. There were several slides, showing the wonderful construction of birds' nests. Some very interesting slides were shown of that wonderful little insect the ant. Miss Wallington afterwards dealt with the homes of other insects, and with the rabbit. A vote of thanks proposed by Mr. N. Hough seconded by Mr. G. Dale was passed thank- ing Miss Wallington 7far her very instructive lecture, and Mr. Wallington fir so ably manipulating the lan- tern.

to that district for the express purpose of getting away from public-houses. (Hear. hear.) The CHAIRMAN pointed out that although the Coun- cil had not approved of the action which had been taken with reference to the Green Dragon licence, still it was the work of a committee under the supervision of the Council. With regard to the question of compensa- tionbeing paid to brewers for licences surrendered, he was of opinion that compensation should be paid to in- dividuals whose property was depreciated by the planting of public-houses in their midst. ' The Chairman's services were acknowledged on the preposition of the Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS, seconded by Mr. THOMAS DALE, and supported by Mr. S. B. STRINGER,. The last-named speaker said one of their most important duties was to purge the Watch Committee. MR. PARKER having replied, the meeting terminated SEAWEED AS A MEDICINE. It is a god-send to sufferers from indigestion, wind, headache, general weakness, kidney trouble, weak and painful back, torpid liver, female troubles, poorness of blood, and HABITUAL CONSTIPATION.
